\Phi(\mathbf{w})=\lim_{\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathbf{w},\mathbf{w}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}^{c}}\Phi(\mathbf{w}^{\prime})\text{ for all }{}\mathbf{w}\in\partial\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon},

the resulting
\Phi
is differentiable to all orders on
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}^{c}\cup\partial\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
(when we define derivatives as the limits coming from outside
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}^{c}
). (Compactness here is important.) As
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}^{c}\cup\partial\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
is closed, applying Whitney’s Extension Theorem as mentioned in
Cattiaux et al. 2013
,
\Phi
above can be extended to a function differentiable to all orders on all of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
so that
(
5.1
)
holds on
\{\mathbf{w}:F(\mathbf{w})\geq\varepsilon\}
. Note
\Phi\geq 1
on
\{\mathbf{w}:F(\mathbf{w})\geq\varepsilon\}
.

Suppose the resulting
\Phi
from the extension was not non-negative. Let
B:=\inf\Phi<0
. Observe
\{\mathbf{w}:\Phi(\mathbf{w})\leq 0\}\subset\{\mathbf{w}:\Phi(\mathbf{w})<\frac{1}{2}\}\subset\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
. Apply the standard construction of bump functions to the compact set
\{\mathbf{w}:\Phi(\mathbf{w})\leq 0\}
contained in the open set
\{\mathbf{w}:\Phi(\mathbf{w})<\frac{1}{2}\}
to obtain a function
\chi
differentiable to all orders supported on
\{\mathbf{w}:\Phi(\mathbf{w})<\frac{1}{2}\}
and identically 1 on
\{\mathbf{w}:\Phi(\mathbf{w})\leq 0\}
. Then
\Phi-B\chi
is non-negative (recall
B<0
) and differentiable to all orders, and is identical to
\Phi
on
\mathcal{A}^{c}_{\varepsilon}
. Taking
\Phi\leftarrow\Phi-B\chi
, this gives us the existence of
\Phi\geq 0
differentiable to all orders where we have its explicit form and know it satisfies
(
5.1
)
and therefore
(
5.1
)
(upon dividing both sides by
\beta>0
) on
\mathcal{A}^{c}_{\varepsilon}
.

To conclude, note from our remarks from
Section
4
that

\tau^{\prime}_{\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}}(\mathbf{w}^{\prime})=\frac{1}{\beta}\tau_{\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}}(\mathbf{w}^{\prime}).

Therefore on
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}^{c}
we can also write

\Phi(\mathbf{w}^{\prime})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}^{\prime}}\left[\exp\left(\lambda\tau_{\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}}\right)\right]\geq 1.

This completes the proof.
∎

Now we prove
Lemma
5.1
.

Lemma 5.1
.

Suppose
F
satisfies
Assumption
2.2
and
\mu_{\beta}
has finite second moment
S<\infty
. Then for
\varepsilon\geq\frac{2d}{\beta}\log(4\pi e\beta LdS)
, we have
\mu_{\beta}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}
.

Proof.
As
F(\mathbf{w})
is non-negative, by Markov’s Inequality, we have

\mu_{\beta}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}^{c}\right)=\mu_{\beta}\left(\{\mathbf{w}:F(\mathbf{w})>\varepsilon\}\right)\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\mu_{\beta}}\left[F(\mathbf{w})\right]}{\varepsilon}.

Now we compute
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\mu_{\beta}}\left[F(\mathbf{w})\right]
with the same strategy as in the proof of Proposition 11 of
Raginsky et al. 2017
. Write

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\mu_{\beta}}\left[F(\mathbf{w})\right]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}F(\mathbf{w})\mu_{\beta}(\mathbf{w})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{w}=\frac{1}{\beta}\left(h(\mu_{\beta})-\log Z\right).

Here
Z
is the partition function of
\mu_{\beta}
and

h(\mu_{\beta})=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mu_{\beta}(\mathbf{w})\log\mu_{\beta}(\mathbf{w})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{w}

is the differential entropy of
\mu_{\beta}
.

To upper bound the differential entropy of
\mu_{\beta}
, we use the same derivation as the proof of Proposition 11 of
Raginsky et al. 2017
. The assumption that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|\mathbf{w}\right\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\beta}(\mathbf{w})\leq S
, as well as the fact that the differential entropy of a measure with finite second moment is upper bounded by the differential entropy of a Gaussian with the same second moment, yields

h(\mu_{\beta})\leq\frac{d}{2}\log\left(\frac{2\pi eS}{d}\right).

Now we aim to lower bound the partition function
Z
. Using
Lemma
8.4
and
Lemma
8.5
, we obtain

\displaystyle\log Z

\displaystyle=\log\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-\beta F(\mathbf{w})}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{w}

\displaystyle\geq\log\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-\beta L\left\|\mathbf{w}-\mathbf{w}^{\star}\right\|^{s+1}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{w}

\displaystyle=\log\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-\beta L\left\|\mathbf{w}\right\|^{s+1}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{w}

\displaystyle=\log\left(\frac{2\pi^{d/2}}{\Gamma(d/2)}\cdot\frac{1}{s+1}\cdot\left(\beta L\right)^{-\frac{d}{s+1}}\cdot\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{s+1}\right)\right).

It is well known that on
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
,
\Gamma(\cdot)
attains a constant lower bound of at least
\frac{1}{2}
(the real value is around 0.8856, but this is all we need for our purposes). Moreover, by well-known properties of
\Gamma(\cdot)
, we have
\Gamma(d/2)=\frac{d}{2}\cdot\frac{d-2}{2}\cdot\cdots\cdot\frac{d-2\lfloor d/2\rfloor+r^{\prime}+2}{2}\cdot\Gamma\left(\frac{d-2\lfloor d/2\rfloor+r^{\prime}}{2}\right)
, where
r^{\prime}=2\left(1-d\pmod{2}\right)
. Since
\frac{d-2\lfloor d/2\rfloor+r^{\prime}}{2}\in\{1/2,1\}
and
\Gamma(1/2)=\sqrt{\pi}
,
\Gamma(1)\leq 1
, this gives
\Gamma\left(\frac{d-2\lfloor d/2\rfloor}{2}\right)\leq d^{d/2}\sqrt{\pi}
. This implies (since
\beta L\geq 1
) the following very loose bound:

\displaystyle\log Z

\displaystyle\geq\log\left(\frac{2\pi^{d/2}}{\Gamma(d/2)}\cdot\frac{1}{s+1}\cdot\left(\beta L\right)^{-\frac{d}{s+1}}\cdot\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{s+1}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\geq\log\left(\frac{\pi^{d/2}}{2\sqrt{\pi}\left(\beta L\right)^{d}d^{d/2}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq-d\log\left(2\beta Ld\right).

Hence, we see

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\mu_{\beta}}\left[F(\mathbf{w})\right]=\frac{1}{\beta}\left(h(\mu_{\beta})-\log Z\right)\leq\frac{d}{\beta}\left(\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\frac{2\pi eS}{d}\right)+\log\left(2\beta Ld\right)\right)\leq\frac{d}{\beta}\log(4\pi e\beta LdS).

The conclusion follows from our condition on
\beta
and the original application of Markov’s Inequality.

Note it suffices to just take
\varepsilon\geq 2\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\mu_{\beta}}\left[F(\mathbf{w})\right]
to make this proof work; most of our work was to find a suitable upper bound for
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\mu_{\beta}}\left[F(\mathbf{w})\right]
. Also,
\varepsilon=\Omega\left(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{w}\sim\mu_{\beta}}\left[F(\mathbf{w})\right]\right)
is necessary, as demonstrated by the Gaussian example in
Subsection
1.1
.
∎

5.2
Proof of
Theorem
3.1

We derive the implication of a rate function
R(\mathbf{w},t)
satisfying the condition
(
3
)
to a geometric condition, which we described in
Section
3
. First we convert
(
3
)
into a more tractable condition about the rate function:

Lemma 5.2
.

Assume
R
has continuous second partials and that

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert\left\langle R(\mathbf{w},t),\nabla F(\mathbf{w})\right\rangle\right\rvert\right],\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert\Delta R(\mathbf{w}(s),t)\right\rvert\right]<\infty,\mathbb{P}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\left\|R(\mathbf{w}(\tau),t)\right\|_{2}^{2}\mathrm{d}\tau<\infty\right)=1.

Then, we have that
R
satisfying
(
3
)
implies the condition:

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}R(\mathbf{w},t)\geq-\langle\nabla R(\mathbf{w},t),\nabla F(\mathbf{w})\rangle+\frac{1}{\beta}\Delta R(\mathbf{w},t)\text{ for all }{}\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},t\geq 0.\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:derivconditionadmissibile}

Proof.
Note from
\mathbb{E}\left[R(\mathbf{w}(s),t)\right]\leq R(\mathbf{w},s+t)\text{ for all }{}s,t,\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{w}(0)
we have

\lim_{s\rightarrow 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[R(\mathbf{w}(s),t)\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[R(\mathbf{w}(0),t)\right]}{s}\leq\lim_{s\rightarrow 0}\frac{R(\mathbf{w},s+t)-R(\mathbf{w},t)}{s}=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}R(\mathbf{w},t).

In the following we consider
t
as a fixed constant. Note
\mathbb{E}\left[R(\mathbf{w}(\cdot),t)\right]
is a deterministic function of the argument and so by definition of partial derivative

\lim_{s\rightarrow 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[R(\mathbf{w}(s),t)\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[R(\mathbf{w}(0),t)\right]}{s}=\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\mathbb{E}\left[R(\mathbf{w}(s),t)\right]\Big|_{s=0}.

Thus the above becomes

\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\mathbb{E}\left[R(\mathbf{w}(s),t)\right]\Big|_{s=0}\leq\frac{\partial}{\partial t}R(\mathbf{w},t).

Now, recall Langevin Dynamics
(
1
)
is given by the SDE

\mathrm{d}\mathbf{w}(s)=-\nabla F(\mathbf{w}(s))\mathrm{d}s+\sqrt{2/\beta}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}(s),

where
\mathbf{B}(s)
is the standard Brownian motion in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\beta
is the inverse temperature parameter. This SDE is a compact way of writing

\mathbf{w}(s)=\mathbf{w}(0)-\int_{0}^{s}\nabla F(\mathbf{w}(\tau))\mathrm{d}\tau+\int_{0}^{s}\sqrt{2/\beta}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}(\tau).

The next step is to find the corresponding Itô process that describes
R(\mathbf{w}(s),t)
. By Itô’s Lemma,

\mathrm{d}R(\mathbf{w}(s),t)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left(\frac{\partial R}{\partial\mathbf{w}_{i}}(\mathbf{w}(s),t)\right)\mathrm{d}\mathbf{w}(s)_{i}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq d}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}R}{\partial\mathbf{w}_{i}\partial\mathbf{w}_{j}}(\mathbf{w}(s),t)\right)\mathrm{d}\mathbf{w}(s)_{i}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{w}(s)_{j}.

Straightforward calculation and the fact that
(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}(s))_{i}(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}(s))_{j}=\delta_{i=j}\mathrm{d}s
gives

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\mathbf{w}(s)_{i}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{w}(s)_{j}

\displaystyle=\left(-\nabla F(\mathbf{w}(s))_{i}\mathrm{d}s+\sqrt{2/\beta}(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}(s))_{i}\right)\left(-\nabla F(\mathbf{w}(s))_{j}\mathrm{d}s+\sqrt{2/\beta}(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}(s))_{j}\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }{}i\neq j\\
\frac{2}{\beta}\mathrm{d}s&\text{ otherwise if }{}i=j.\end{cases}

Substituting this into the above we get

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}R(\mathbf{w}(s),t)

\displaystyle=\left\langle\nabla R(\mathbf{w}(s),t),\mathrm{d}\mathbf{w}(s)\right\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}R}{\partial\mathbf{w}_{i}^{2}}(\mathbf{w}(s),t)\right)\frac{2}{\beta}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\left\langle\nabla R(\mathbf{w}(s),t),\left(-\nabla F(\mathbf{w}(s))\mathrm{d}s+\sqrt{2/\beta}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}(s)\right)\right\rangle+\frac{1}{\beta}\Delta R(\mathbf{w}(s),t)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=-\left\langle\nabla R(\mathbf{w}(s),t),\nabla F(\mathbf{w}(s))\right\rangle+\frac{1}{\beta}\Delta R(\mathbf{w}(s),t)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\sqrt{2/\beta}\left\langle\nabla R(\mathbf{w}(s),t),\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}(s)\right\rangle.

We can rewrite this as

\displaystyle R(\mathbf{w}(s),t)

\displaystyle=R(\mathbf{w}(0),t)+\int_{0}^{s}\left(-\left\langle\nabla R(\mathbf{w}(\tau),t),\nabla F(\mathbf{w}(\tau))\right\rangle+\frac{1}{\beta}\Delta R(\mathbf{w}(\tau),t)\right)\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\int_{0}^{s}\sqrt{2/\beta}\left\langle\nabla R(\mathbf{w}(\tau),t),\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}(\tau)\right\rangle.

We aim to find an expression for
\mathbb{E}\left[R(\mathbf{w}(s)),t\right]
. Note by our conditions, considering Definition 3.1.4, Theorem 3.2.1 and Definition 3.3.2 together in
Øksendal 2003
, we see that in fact we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{s}\sqrt{2/\beta}\left\langle\nabla R(\mathbf{w}(\tau),t),\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}(\tau)\right\rangle\right]=0\text{ for all }{}s\geq 0.

Thus taking expectations gives

\mathbb{E}\left[R(\mathbf{w}(s),t)\right]=R(\mathbf{w}(0),t)+\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{s}\left(-\left\langle\nabla R(\mathbf{w}(\tau),t),\nabla F(\mathbf{w}(\tau))\right\rangle+\frac{1}{\beta}\Delta R(\mathbf{w}(\tau),t)\right)\mathrm{d}\tau\right].

Now we want to deal with this expectation. By Dominated Convergence, thanks to our assumptions, we can swap the order of expectation and integration. So we obtain

\mathbb{E}\left[R(\mathbf{w}(s),t)\right]=R(\mathbf{w}(0),t)+\int_{0}^{s}\mathbb{E}\left[-\left\langle\nabla R(\mathbf{w}(\tau),t),\nabla F(\mathbf{w}(\tau))\right\rangle+\frac{1}{\beta}\Delta R(\mathbf{w}(\tau),t)\right]\mathrm{d}\tau.

Now applying Leibniz Rule gives

\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\mathbb{E}\left[R(\mathbf{w}(s),t)\right]=\frac{\partial}{\partial s}(s)\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[-\left\langle\nabla R(\mathbf{w}(s),t),\nabla F(\mathbf{w}(s))\right\rangle+\frac{1}{\beta}\Delta R(\mathbf{w}(s),t)\right].

And thus our condition becomes

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}R(\mathbf{w},t)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\mathbb{E}\left[R(\mathbf{w}(s),t)\right]\Big|_{s=0}

\displaystyle=\lim_{s\rightarrow 0}\mathbb{E}\left[-\left\langle\nabla R(\mathbf{w}(s),t),\nabla F(\mathbf{w}(s))\right\rangle+\frac{1}{\beta}\Delta R(\mathbf{w}(s),t)\right]

\displaystyle=-\langle\nabla R(\mathbf{w},t),\nabla F(\mathbf{w})\rangle+\frac{1}{\beta}\Delta R(\mathbf{w},t).

This last step is justified as follows. Our formula for
\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\mathbb{E}\left[R(\mathbf{w}(s),t)\right]
holds for all
s>0
, and our expression for
\mathbb{E}\left[R(\mathbf{w}(s),t)\right]
is continuous in
s
. Recall our assumptions that
R
has continuous second partials and

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert\left\langle\nabla R(\mathbf{w},t),\nabla F(\mathbf{w})\right\rangle\right\rvert\right],\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert\Delta R(\mathbf{w}(s),t)\right\rvert\right]<\infty.

Thus, Dominated Convergence Theorem may be used to swap the order of limit and expectation, so we may take the limit of both sides as
s\rightarrow 0^{+}
, yielding

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}R(\mathbf{w},t)

\displaystyle\geq-\mathbb{E}[\left\langle\nabla R(\mathbf{w},t),\nabla F(\mathbf{w})\right\rangle]+\frac{1}{\beta}\Delta R(\mathbf{w},t)

\displaystyle=-\langle\nabla R(\mathbf{w},t),\nabla F(\mathbf{w})\rangle+\frac{1}{\beta}\Delta R(\mathbf{w},t),

where the expectation clearly drops since we took the limit.

To justify the application of Dominated Convergence in more detail, note
\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{w}(0)
and
t
here are both fixed and so for
s>0
small enough, we have

\left\lvert\left\langle\nabla R(\mathbf{w}(s),t),\nabla F(\mathbf{w}(s)\right\rangle\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert\left\langle\nabla R(\mathbf{w},t),\nabla F(\mathbf{w})\right\rangle\right\rvert+1,

by continuity of the gradients of
R(\mathbf{w},t)
and
F
. Now under the expectation with respect to the probability measure given by the Brownian motion up to time
s
we get

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert\left\langle\nabla R(\mathbf{w}(s),t),\nabla F(\mathbf{w}(s)\right\rangle\right\rvert\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert\left\langle\nabla R(\mathbf{w},t),\nabla F(\mathbf{w})\right\rangle\right\rvert\right]+1.

The same argument, since we have the appropriate conditions, can be used for
\Delta R
.
∎

Recall that we claimed in
Section
3
that a rate function
R(\mathbf{w},t)
satisfying
(
3
)
implied
\Phi(\mathbf{w})=\int_{0}^{\infty}R(\mathbf{w},t)\mathrm{d}t
satisfies the definition of admissible potential
(
3.1
)
. Using
Lemma
5.2
, we show this now.

Theorem 5.4

(Constructing an admissible potential; analogy to Theorem 2 from
De Sa et al. 2022
)
.

Assuming the conditions

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert\left\langle\nabla R(\mathbf{w},t),\nabla F(\mathbf{w})\right\rangle\right\rvert\right]<\infty,\left\|\int_{0}^{\infty}\nabla R(\mathbf{w},t)\mathrm{d}t\right\|<\infty,\int_{0}^{\infty}R(\mathbf{w},t)\mathrm{d}t<\infty

for all
\mathbf{w}
, and the assumption that
R(\mathbf{w},t)
and
F
have continuous gradients. Then we know that

\Phi(\mathbf{w})=\int_{0}^{\infty}R(\mathbf{w},t)\mathrm{d}t

is an admissable potential if
R(\mathbf{w},t)
satisfies the relationship
(
5.2
)
given in
Lemma
5.2
.

Proof.
Rearrange the condition from
Lemma
5.2
to read

-\frac{\partial}{\partial t}R(\mathbf{w},t)+\frac{1}{\beta}\Delta R(\mathbf{w},t)\leq\left\langle\nabla R(\mathbf{w},t),\nabla F(\mathbf{w})\right\rangle.

Next integrate both sides from
0\leq t<\infty
, yielding

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(-\frac{\partial}{\partial t}R(\mathbf{w},t)+\frac{1}{\beta}\Delta R(\mathbf{w},t))\right)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\left\langle\nabla R(\mathbf{w},t),\nabla F(\mathbf{w})\right\rangle\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\left\langle\int_{0}^{\infty}\nabla R(\mathbf{w},t)\mathrm{d}t,\nabla F(\mathbf{w})\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\nabla\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}R(\mathbf{w},t)\mathrm{d}t\right),\nabla F(\mathbf{w})\right\rangle.

The last step follows as for all
1\leq i\leq d
we have again by Dominated Convergence Theorem that

\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{w}_{i}}\int_{0}^{\infty}R(\mathbf{w},t)\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{w}_{i}}R(\mathbf{w},t)\mathrm{d}t,

by assumption that
\int_{0}^{\infty}R(\mathbf{w},t)\mathrm{d}t<\infty\text{ for all }{}\mathbf{w}
(as
R
is non-negative).

Next, observe that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(-\frac{\partial}{\partial t}R(\mathbf{w},t)+\frac{1}{\beta}\Delta R(\mathbf{w},t))\right)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}-\frac{\partial}{\partial t}R(\mathbf{w},t)\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{\beta}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Delta R(\mathbf{w},t)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=R(\mathbf{w},0)+\frac{1}{\beta}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Delta R(\mathbf{w},t)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w})+\frac{1}{\beta}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Delta R(\mathbf{w},t)\mathrm{d}t,

since
\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}R(\mathbf{w},t)=0
and as
R(\mathbf{w},0)\geq F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w})
. To complete the proof, note by two applications of Dominated Convergence Theorem that

\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\mathbf{w}_{i}^{2}}R(\mathbf{w},t)\mathrm{d}t=\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{w}_{i}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{w}_{i}}R(\mathbf{w},t)\mathrm{d}t=\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\mathbf{w}_{i}^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}R(\mathbf{w},t)\mathrm{d}t,

by assumption that
\|\int_{0}^{\infty}\nabla R(\mathbf{w},t)\mathrm{d}t\|<\infty
and
\int_{0}^{\infty}R(\mathbf{w},t)\mathrm{d}t<\infty\text{ for all }{}\mathbf{w}
(as
R
is non-negative). Therefore we have

\int_{0}^{\infty}\Delta R(\mathbf{w},t)\mathrm{d}t=\Delta\int_{0}^{\infty}R(\mathbf{w},t)\mathrm{d}t

and so by definition of the potential
\Phi
we have

\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}),\nabla F(\mathbf{w})\right\rangle\geq F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w})+\frac{1}{\beta}\Delta\Phi(\mathbf{w}),

as wanted.
∎

Recall in
Section
3
we stated
(
3.1
)
implies that the Langevin Diffusion succeeds as an optimization strategy for rate in expectation. Here we show this.

Theorem 5.5

(Getting a rate; analogy to Theorem 1 from
De Sa et al. 2022
)
.

Suppose that we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w},t),\nabla F(\mathbf{w})\right\rangle\right\rvert\right],\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert\Delta\Phi(\mathbf{w},t)\right\rvert\right]<\infty

for all
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, for an admissable potential
\Phi
with respect to
F
. Then running Langevin Dynamics at temperature
\beta
starting from
\mathbf{w}(0)
, we have that

\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\left[F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}(s))\right]\mathrm{d}s\leq\frac{\Phi(\mathbf{w}(0))}{t}.

That is, if we uniformly choose a stopping time in
[0,t]
we obtain a
O(t^{-1})
rate.

Proof.
Considering an arbitrary path of
SGLD
and then taking expectation with respect to the Brownian motion, the admissability condition
(
3.1
)
rearranges to

\mathbb{E}\left[F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}(s))\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}(s)),\nabla F(\mathbf{w}(s))\right\rangle-\frac{1}{\beta}\Delta\Phi(\mathbf{w}(s))\right]\text{ for all }{}s\geq 0,

because we have such an inequality pointwise by definition of admissability.

By an analogous application of Itô’s formula as the above in the proof of
Lemma
5.2
, since our conditions allow us to apply Dominated Convergence Theorem, we can compute

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\mathbb{E}\left[\Phi(\mathbf{w}(s))\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[-\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}(s)),\nabla F(\mathbf{w}(s))\right\rangle+\frac{1}{\beta}\Delta\Phi(\mathbf{w}(s))\right],

and so the condition actually becomes

\mathbb{E}\left[F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}(s))\right]\leq-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\mathbb{E}\left[\Phi(\mathbf{w}(s))\right].

Integrating this from all
0\leq s\leq t
gives

\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\left[F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}(s))\right]\mathrm{d}s\leq-\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\mathbb{E}\left[\Phi(\mathbf{w}(s))\right]\mathrm{d}s\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\Phi(\mathbf{w}(0))\right]=\Phi(\mathbf{w}(0)).

Dividing by
t
yields the result.
∎

6
Proofs for
Section
3

In this section we prove our results with constant probability guarantees; we can recover our results from
Section
3
easily via the standard log-boosting trick. Moreover, in this section,
\rho_{\Phi}(z)
is defined in terms of
\rho_{\Phi,1}(z),\rho_{\Phi,2}(z),\rho_{\Phi,3}(z)
as i
Lemma
6.1
(to be stated later in this section) for the general
p
case.

6.1
Proof of
Theorem
3.2

In the exact gradient oracle setting, we have the following result for optimization in discrete time. This is a formal statement of
Theorem
3.2
.

Theorem 6.1
.

Consider
F
and suppose
F
is differentiable. Suppose that we have
(
3.1
)
:

\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}),\nabla F(\mathbf{w})\right\rangle\geq F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w})+\frac{1}{\beta}\Delta\Phi(\mathbf{w}),

for some
\beta>0
. Suppose
Assumption
2.2
,
3.1
, and
3.2
hold. Moreover suppose
\beta\geq d\sqrt{\frac{\log 10}{C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}}
where
C(\cdot)
is defined below.

Define the following quantities:

A_{0}(\mathbf{w}_{0})=\theta\left(\rho_{\Phi}^{-1}\left(\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)\right)\right)-\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)>0,A_{1}(\mathbf{w}_{0})=12\sqrt{2}CB_{\textsc{grad}}^{3}.

Here
C
comes from
Lemma
6.2
and
B_{\textsc{grad}}:=L\left(R_{1}+\left\|\mathbf{w}^{\star}\right\|\right)^{s}
,
L
comes from
Assumption
2.2
and
R_{1}
comes from
Assumption
3.2
. (If necessary take
C\leftarrow\max(C,1)
, and
B_{\textsc{grad}}\leftarrow\max(B_{\textsc{grad}},1)
.) Now define

r(\mathbf{w}_{0})=\min\left(1,\frac{3}{4C},\frac{1}{B_{\textsc{grad}}}\right),C(\mathbf{w}_{0})=\min\left(1,\frac{A_{0}(\mathbf{w}_{0})^{2}r(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{128A_{1}(\mathbf{w}_{0})^{2}}\right).

In terms of these define (where
\theta
comes from
Lemma
6.1
),

M(\mathbf{w}_{0})=\frac{10\max\left(\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right),6CB_{\textsc{grad}}^{3}\right)}{\theta^{\prime}\left(\rho_{\Phi}^{-1}\left(\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)\right)\right)}\in(0,\infty).

Algorithm 1

Discrete Time Gradient Langevin Dynamics with slight modifications

1:

if

\varepsilon\leq\min\left(1/e,C^{\prime},\sqrt{\frac{C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{\log 10}}\right)
, where
C^{\prime}
is an absolute, dimension and temperature free constant given in
Lemma
6.3
:
then

2:

Consider some constant choice of
\eta
,
T
given in
Lemma
6.3
and run the following process with
\eta
for
T
steps:

\mathbf{w}_{t+1}\leftarrow\mathbf{w}_{t}-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}.

Here we sample
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\sim\sqrt{d}\mathcal{S}^{d-1}
uniformly (in spirit a Gaussian).

3:

else

if

\varepsilon>\min\left(1/e,C^{\prime},\sqrt{\frac{C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{\log 10}}\right)
:
then

4:

Run this process with
\varepsilon\leftarrow\min\left(1/e,C^{\prime},\sqrt{\frac{C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{\log 10}}\right)
.

Now consider running
Algorithm
1
. We claim it has the following guarantees. First, its runtime
T
is as follows:

1.

If
\varepsilon\leq\min\left(1/e,C^{\prime},\sqrt{\frac{C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{\log 10}}\right)
: then

T\leq\begin{cases}\frac{\beta^{2}}{d^{2}}&\text{ if }{}\beta\leq\frac{d}{\varepsilon/\left(\log 1/\varepsilon\right)^{2}}\\
\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\log 1/\varepsilon\right)^{2}&\text{ if }{}\beta\geq\frac{d}{\varepsilon/\left(\log 1/\varepsilon\right)^{2}}.\end{cases}

2.

If
\varepsilon>\min\left(1/e,C^{\prime},\sqrt{\frac{C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{\log 10}}\right)
: then we have the same runtime guarantee as implied by above with
\min\left(1/e,C^{\prime},\sqrt{\frac{C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{\log 10}}\right)
in place of
\varepsilon
.

In terms of error, we have with probability at least 0.75 (taken over the
\{\varepsilon_{t}\}_{0\leq t\leq T-1}
) that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{t})\leq\begin{cases}6M(\mathbf{w}_{0})\left(\frac{\log(20)+2\log(\beta/d)}{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}+1\right)\frac{d}{\beta}&\text{ if }{}\beta\leq\frac{d}{\varepsilon/(\log 1/\varepsilon)^{2}}\\
6M(\mathbf{w}_{0})\left(\frac{1}{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}+1\right)\varepsilon&\text{ if }{}\beta\geq\frac{d}{\varepsilon/(\log 1/\varepsilon)^{2}}.\end{cases}

Here
C^{\prime}
is an absolute, dimension and temperature free constant given in
Lemma
6.3
.

Note now that logarithmic boosting tricks proves that a given one of these guarantees can occur with probability at least
1-\delta
using at most
T\log(1/\delta)
steps.

Remark 6.1
.

In our proofs of
Theorem
3.2
,
3.3
, and
3.4
, our results hold under the more general condition

\left\langle\nabla F(\mathbf{w}),\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w})\right\rangle-\frac{1}{\beta}\Delta F(\mathbf{w})\geq A(\mathbf{w}),

for a general non-negative cost function
A(\mathbf{w})
. Note
A(\mathbf{w})
need not be continuous (for example,
F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w})
is not continuous).
This lets us use the Lemmas we develop here, in the proofs of
Theorem
2.2
,
2.3
, and
2.4
.

The proofs of this result is ‘optimization style’. We break it into parts. First we perform a one-step discretization bound in expectation by applying
Lemma
6.1
, which gives
Lemma
6.2
. Then we analyze a stochastic process naturally arising from this setup to show that
\Phi
indeed is a potential function for the discrete-time algorithm, for appropriate choice of
\eta
and
T
, which is detailed in
Lemma
6.3
. After this, we can conclude upon using the resulting bound and telescoping.

First, we need to show that with self-bounding regularity, by composing with the appropriate function, we can obtain some analogue of third-order smoothness in order to perform optimization-style discretization. We detail this as follows via the following Lemmas, which are also used later to prove
Theorem
7.1
.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
\Phi
be any non-negative function that satisfies polynomial self-bounding regularity to first, second, and third orders
7
7

7

This implicitly assumes
\Phi
is differentiable through third order.
, that is we have
\left\|\nabla^{i}\Phi(\mathbf{w})\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}\leq\rho_{\Phi,i}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w})\right)
for
1\leq i\leq 3
, where
\rho_{\Phi,i}(z)=\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}c_{i,j}z^{d_{i,j}}
for all
z\geq 0
(where all the
d_{i,j}\geq 0
). Then there exists some
\theta:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that
\theta^{\prime}(z)>0
,
\theta^{\prime\prime}(z)<0
,
\theta^{\prime\prime\prime}(z)\geq 0
for all
z\geq 0
, and

\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{u})\right)\leq\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w})\right)+\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w})\right)\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}),\mathbf{u}\right\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w})\right)\left\langle\nabla^{2}\Phi(\mathbf{w})\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}\right\rangle+\frac{C}{6}\left\|\mathbf{u}\right\|^{3},

for some constant
C
that depends only on the form of the functions
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
, and
\rho_{3}
.

Moreover, we also have

\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{u})\right)\leq\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w})\right)+\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w})\right)\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}),\mathbf{u}\right\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\left\|\mathbf{u}\right\|^{2},

and

\left\|\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w})\right\|\leq\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w})\right)\sqrt{2\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w})\right)}.

Proof.
Note we can assume without loss of generality that all the
c_{i,j}\geq 0
, and thus again we can assume without loss of generality that for all
z\geq 0
we have

\max\left(\rho_{\Phi,1}(z),\rho_{\Phi,1}(z)^{3},\rho_{\Phi,2}(z),\rho_{\Phi,3}(z),\rho_{\Phi,1}(z)\rho_{\Phi,2}(z)\right)\leq A+Az^{p}\leq 2A(z+1)^{p}

for some
A\geq 0,p\geq 0
. The last step follows from
Lemma
8.6
.

Next, define
\rho_{\Phi}(z):=2A(z+1)^{p}
, which is clearly non-negative and increasing. Thus for all
z\geq 0
we have

\rho_{\Phi}(z)\geq\max\left(\rho_{\Phi,1}(z),\rho_{\Phi,1}(z)^{3},\rho_{\Phi,2}(z),\rho_{\Phi,3}(z),\rho_{\Phi,1}(z)\rho_{\Phi,2}(z)\right).

Now let
\theta(z)
be defined by
\theta^{\prime}(z)=\frac{1}{\rho_{\Phi}(z)}
and
\theta(0)=0
. The potential
\Phi
we consider is non-negative and so we only consider
z\geq 0
; thus,
\theta
is differentiable to all orders. Clearly
\theta^{\prime}(z)>0
. We can also check that
\theta^{\prime\prime}(z)=-\frac{p}{2A}(z+1)^{-p-1}<0
, thus

\left\lvert\theta^{\prime\prime}(z)\right\rvert\rho_{\Phi}(z)=\frac{p}{2A}(z+1)^{-p-1}\cdot 2A(z+1)^{p}\leq p(z+1)^{-1}\leq p.

for all
z\geq 0
. Finally, we can compute
\theta^{\prime\prime\prime}(z)=\frac{p(p+1)}{2A}(z+1)^{-p-2}
, thus

\left\lvert\theta^{\prime\prime\prime}(z)\right\rvert\rho_{\Phi}(z)=\theta^{\prime\prime\prime}(z)\rho_{\Phi}(z)=\frac{p(p+1)}{2A}(z+1)^{-p-2}\cdot 2A(z+1)^{p}=\frac{p(p+1)}{(z+1)^{2}}\leq p(p+1)

for all
z\geq 0
.

Now define for all
0\leq\alpha\leq 1
,

l(\alpha):=\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u})\right).

Recall
\Phi
is non-negative, so all the inputs here to
\theta
are non-negative.
l(\alpha)
is differentiable to third order, since
\Phi
is and
\theta
is for non-negative inputs.

By standard calculation using the Chain Rule (this is also done in the proof of Lemma 11 of
De Sa et al. 2022
),

\displaystyle l^{\prime}(\alpha)=\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u})\right)\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u}),\mathbf{u}\right\rangle.

We also have, from similar calculation (also done in the proof of Lemma 11 of
De Sa et al. 2022
) and using that
\theta^{\prime\prime}(z)\leq 0
for all
z\geq 0
which was established earlier,

\displaystyle l^{\prime\prime}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\theta^{\prime\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u})\right)\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u}),\mathbf{u}\right\rangle^{2}+\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u})\right)\left\langle\nabla^{2}\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u})\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u})\right)\left\langle\nabla^{2}\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u})\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}\right\rangle.

Similar calculation, noting
\theta^{\prime}(z)\geq 0
and the bounds we established earlier on
\left\lvert\theta^{\prime\prime}(z)\right\rvert\rho_{\Phi}(z)
and
\left\lvert\theta^{\prime\prime\prime}(z)\right\rvert\rho_{\Phi}(z)
, gives

\displaystyle l^{\prime\prime\prime}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\theta^{\prime\prime\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u})\right)\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u}),\mathbf{u}\right\rangle\cdot\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u}),\mathbf{u}\right\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\theta^{\prime\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u})\right)\cdot 2\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u}),\mathbf{u}\right\rangle\left\langle\nabla^{2}\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u})\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\theta^{\prime\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u})\right)\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u}),\mathbf{u}\right\rangle\cdot\left\langle\nabla^{2}\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u})\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u})\right)\nabla^{3}\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u})[\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}]

\displaystyle=\theta^{\prime\prime\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u})\right)\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u}),\mathbf{u}\right\rangle^{3}

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+3\theta^{\prime\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u})\right)\left\langle\nabla^{2}\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u})\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}\right\rangle\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u}),\mathbf{u}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u})\right)\nabla^{3}\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u})[\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}]

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert\theta^{\prime\prime\prime}(\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u}))\right\rvert\rho_{\Phi,1}(\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u}))^{3}\left\|\mathbf{u}\right\|^{3}

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+3\left\lvert\theta^{\prime\prime}(\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u}))\right\rvert\rho_{\Phi,1}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u})\right)\rho_{\Phi,2}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u})\right)\left\|\mathbf{u}\right\|^{3}

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\theta^{\prime}(\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u}))\rho_{\Phi,3}(\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u}))\left\|\mathbf{u}\right\|^{3}

\displaystyle\leq\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u})\right)\left(\left\lvert\theta^{\prime\prime\prime}(\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u}))\right\rvert+3\left\lvert\theta^{\prime\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u})\right)\right\rvert+\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\alpha\mathbf{u})\right)\right)\left\|\mathbf{u}\right\|^{3}

\displaystyle\leq(p^{2}+p+3p+1)\left\|\mathbf{u}\right\|^{3}.

From here, we consider Taylor expansion of
l(1)
around
0
. By Taylor’s formula for the remainder, we know for some
\alpha\in[0,1]
that

\displaystyle l(1)

\displaystyle=l(0)+l^{\prime}(0)+\frac{1}{2}l^{\prime\prime}(0)+\frac{1}{6}l^{\prime\prime\prime}(\alpha).

Plugging in the above inequalities, we get

\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{u})\right)\leq\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w})\right)+\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w})\right)\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}),\mathbf{u}\right\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w})\right)\left\langle\nabla^{2}\Phi(\mathbf{w})\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}\right\rangle+\frac{p^{2}+4p+1}{6}\left\|\mathbf{u}\right\|^{3}.

The result follows since
C=p^{2}+4p+1
only depends on the form of the functions
\rho_{\Phi,1},\rho_{\Phi,2}
, and
\rho_{\Phi,3}
.

The second part follows from noticing that
\rho_{\Phi}
as defined here is an upper bound on
\rho_{\Phi,1}
and
\rho_{\Phi,2}
, so the same derivation as in the proof of Lemma 11 of
De Sa et al. 2022
suffices.

Finally, if
\max_{j}\left(d_{i,j}\right)\leq 1
for all
1\leq i\leq 3
(i.e. the max degree of the self-bounding regularity functions is at most 1), we can be a bit tighter in how we define
\theta
. Instead we can just say

\max\left(\rho_{\Phi,1}(z),\rho_{\Phi,1}(z),\rho_{\Phi,1}(z)\right)\leq A+Az^{p}\leq 2A(z+1)^{p}

where
0\leq p\leq 1
, and we define
\rho_{\Phi}(z)=A(z+1)^{p}
. Defining
\theta
by
\theta^{\prime}(z)=\frac{1}{\rho_{\Phi}(z)}
,
\theta(0)=0
analogously as before, note we have for any
z\geq 0
that

\theta^{\prime}(z)>0,\theta^{\prime\prime}(z)<0,\theta^{\prime\prime\prime}(z)>0,

\left\lvert\theta^{\prime\prime\prime}(z)\right\rvert\rho_{\Phi,1}(z)^{3}=\frac{p(p+1)}{A}(z+1)^{-p-2}\cdot 8A^{3}(z+1)^{3p}=8A^{2}p(p+1)(z+1)^{2p-2}\leq 8A^{2}p(p+1),

\left\lvert\theta^{\prime\prime}(z)\right\rvert\rho_{\Phi,1}(z)\rho_{\Phi,2}(z)=\frac{p}{A}(z+1)^{-p-1}\cdot 4A^{2}(z+1)^{2p}=4Ap(z+1)^{p-1}\leq 4Ap,

\left\lvert\theta^{\prime}(z)\right\rvert\rho_{\Phi,3}(z)=\frac{1}{A(z+1)^{p}}\cdot 2A(z+1)^{p}=2.

The above three lines all use
p\leq 1
in the last inequality of those lines. Therefore, an analogous derivation as above gives

\displaystyle\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{u})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w})\right)+\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w})\right)\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}),\mathbf{u}\right\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w})\right)\left\langle\nabla^{2}\Phi(\mathbf{w})\mathbf{u},\mathbf{u}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\frac{4A^{2}p(p+1)+2Ap+1}{3}\left\|\mathbf{u}\right\|^{3}.

∎

Lemma 6.2
.

For one iteration of
GLD
starting at arbitrary
\mathbf{w}_{t}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}}\left[\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)-\eta\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{t})

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\frac{1}{2}\eta^{2}\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{2}+\frac{2C}{3}\eta^{3}\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{3}+2C(\eta d/\beta)^{3/2},

where
p
and
C
are defined from
Lemma
6.1
.

Proof.
First, apply
Lemma
6.1
with
\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{w}_{t}
and
\mathbf{u}=-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}
to obtain

\displaystyle\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right)

\displaystyle=\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t}-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)+\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t}),-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\rangle

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\left\langle\nabla^{2}\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\left(-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right),-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\frac{C}{6}\left\|-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|^{3}

where
C
is defined in the proof of
Lemma
6.1
.

We take expectations of this inequality with respect to
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}
. Let’s consider what each term of the upper bound becomes when we take expectations.

•

First order term: Since
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}
has mean as the 0 vector,

\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}}\left[\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t}),-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\rangle\right]=-\eta\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t}),\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\rangle.

•

Second order term: Note

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}}\left[\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\left\langle\nabla^{2}\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})(-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}),-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\rangle\right]

\displaystyle=\eta^{2}\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\left\langle\nabla^{2}\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t}),\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt-2\eta\left(2\eta/\beta\right)^{1/2}\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\left\langle\nabla^{2}\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t}),\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}}\left[\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right]\right\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\left(2\eta/\beta\right)\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(w_{t})\right)\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}}\left[\left\langle\nabla^{2}\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\rangle\right].

In the above, the cross terms cancel because
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}
has mean of the 0 vector.

Now, consider
\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}}\left[\left\langle\nabla^{2}\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\rangle\right]
. We perform similar analysis as in the derivation and application of Ito’s Lemma. This is where we see how the Laplacian term here actually helps. To make the parallels and motivation to Stochastic Calculus clear, here
\eta
corresponds to
\mathrm{d}t
, and
\sqrt{\eta}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t})_{i}
corresponds to
(\mathrm{d}\mathbf{B}_{t})_{i}
. Note

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}}\left[\left\langle\nabla^{2}\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\rangle\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq d}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}}\left[(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t})_{i}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t})_{j}\left(\nabla^{2}\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)_{ij}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq d}\nabla^{2}\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})_{ij}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}}\left[(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t})_{i}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t})_{j}\right].

We break into cases:

1.

When
i\neq j
: Note by symmetry of the unit sphere that

\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}}\left[(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t})_{i}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t})_{j}\right]=0.

In particular this follows because for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{S}^{d-1}
,
(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t})_{j}
has equal probability of being
\mathbf{x}
or
-\mathbf{x}
.

2.

When
i=j
: This is where we pick up the Laplacian. Note by symmetry,

\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}}\left[(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t})^{2}_{i}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}}\left[(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t})^{2}_{j}\right]\text{ for all }{}i,j,\text{ and }d=\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}}[\sum_{i=1}^{d}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t})^{2}_{i}]=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}}\left[(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t})^{2}_{i}\right].

Therefore,

\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}}\left[(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t})^{2}_{i}\right]=1\text{ for all }{}1\leq i\leq d.

Hence, we obtain the Laplacian
\Delta\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})
: we have plugging this into the above that

\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}}\left[\left\langle\nabla^{2}\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\rangle\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{d}(\nabla^{2}\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t}))_{ii}=\Delta\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t}).

Hence,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}}\left[\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\left\langle\nabla^{2}\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})(-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}),-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\rangle\right]

\displaystyle=\eta^{2}\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\left\langle\nabla^{2}\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t}),\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\rangle+(2\eta/\beta)\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\Delta\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t}).

•

Third order term: By AM-GM, we can prove for all
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\left\|\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{b}\right\|^{3}\leq\left(\left\|\mathbf{a}\right\|+\|\mathbf{b}\|\right)^{3}\leq 4\left\|\mathbf{a}\right\|^{3}+4\|\mathbf{b}\|^{3}.

Thus using this inequality pointwise we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}}\left[\left\|-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|^{3}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 4\eta^{3}\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{3}+4(2\eta/\beta)^{3/2}d^{3/2}.

The last step is because deterministically
\left\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|\leq\sqrt{d}
always.

Using the geometric property
(
3.1
)
and
\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\geq 0
from
Lemma
6.1
,

-\eta\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t}),\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\rangle\leq-\eta\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\left(F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\frac{1}{\beta}\Delta\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right).

Putting these together, this gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}}\left[\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)-\eta\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t}),\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\frac{1}{2}\left(\eta^{2}\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\left\langle\nabla^{2}\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t}),\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\rangle+(2\eta/\beta)\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\Delta\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\frac{C}{6}(4\eta^{3}\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{3}+4(2\eta/\beta)^{3/2}d^{3/2})

\displaystyle\leq\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)-\eta\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\left(F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\frac{1}{\beta}\Delta\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\frac{1}{2}\left(\eta^{2}\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\left\langle\nabla^{2}\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t}),\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\rangle+(2\eta/\beta)\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\Delta\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\frac{C}{6}\left(4\eta^{3}\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{3}+4(2\eta/\beta)^{3/2}d^{3/2}\right).

Note the terms
\eta\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\cdot\frac{1}{\beta}\Delta\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})
and
\frac{1}{2}(2\eta/\beta)\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\Delta\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})
cancel out. Moreover, note by definition of operator norm and since we set
\theta^{\prime}(z)=\frac{1}{\rho_{\Phi}(z)}\leq\frac{1}{\rho_{\Phi,2}(z)}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\eta^{2}\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\left\langle\nabla^{2}\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t}),\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\eta^{2}\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{2}\rho_{2}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\eta^{2}\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{2}.

Thus our above bound becomes

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}}\left[\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)-\eta\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{t})

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\frac{1}{2}\eta^{2}\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{2}+\frac{2C}{3}\eta^{3}\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{3}+2C(\eta d/\beta)^{3/2}.

This is the desired result.
∎

The above result gives us a way to upper bound
\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)
. To control this we will need to control the
\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})
which we do as follows.

Lemma 6.3
.

Suppose
\beta\geq d
and
\varepsilon\leq 1/e
. Additionally suppose
\varepsilon<C^{\prime}
where
z=\frac{1}{C^{\prime}}
is the largest solution to
\frac{\log(\sqrt{10}z(\log z))}{\log^{2}(z)}=1/2
(the existence of finitely many such
C^{\prime}>0
is obvious).

With probability at least
0.9
, we have that

\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\leq\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)

for all
0\leq t\leq T-1
if
T,\eta
are chosen as follows, where
\kappa^{\prime}
comes from
Assumption
3.2
. First define

A_{0}(\mathbf{w}_{0})=\theta\left(\rho_{\Phi}^{-1}\left(\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)\right)\right)-\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)>0,A_{1}(\mathbf{w}_{0})=12\sqrt{2}CB_{\textsc{grad}}^{3}.

Here
C
comes from
Lemma
6.2
and
B_{\textsc{grad}}:=L\left(R_{1}+\left\|\mathbf{w}^{\star}\right\|\right)^{s}
,
L
comes from
Assumption
2.2
and
R_{1}
comes from
Assumption
3.2
. (Again if necessary take
C\leftarrow\max(C,1)
and
B_{\textsc{grad}}\leftarrow\max(B_{\textsc{grad}},1)
.)

Also define

r(\mathbf{w}_{0})=\min\left(1,\frac{3}{4C},\frac{1}{B_{\textsc{grad}}}\right),C(\mathbf{w}_{0})=\min\left(1,\frac{A_{0}(\mathbf{w}_{0})^{2}r(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{128A_{1}(\mathbf{w}_{0})^{2}}\right).

Now we choose
T,\eta
based on cases:

1.

If
\beta\leq\frac{d}{\varepsilon/\left(\log 1/\varepsilon\right)^{2}}
: Take
T,\eta
such that

\eta=r(\mathbf{w}_{0})\frac{d}{\beta}

and where
T
is the floor of the unique solution to the equation

z\log(10z)=C(\mathbf{w}_{0})\frac{\beta^{2}}{d^{2}}.

Existence and uniqueness to this equation is clear since
z\log(10z)
is surjective on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and for every positive real
t
, exactly one positive real
z
is such that
z\log(10z)=t
. Now note if
C(\mathbf{w}_{0})\cdot\frac{\beta^{2}}{d^{2}}\geq 1
then this means
T\leq C(\mathbf{w}_{0})\frac{\beta^{2}}{d^{2}}\leq\frac{\beta^{2}}{d^{2}}
, and otherwise we have
T<1\implies T=0
. However, recall
\beta\geq d
so in all cases we have
T\leq\frac{\beta^{2}}{d^{2}}
.

Also note this means

\eta\leq\min\left(1,\frac{3}{4C},\frac{1}{B_{\textsc{grad}}},\frac{d}{\beta}\right).

2.

If
\beta\geq\frac{d}{\varepsilon/\left(\log 1/\varepsilon\right)^{2}}
: Take
T,\eta
such that

\eta=r(\mathbf{w}_{0})\frac{\varepsilon}{\left(\log 1/\varepsilon\right)^{2}},T=\lfloor\frac{C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\log 1/\varepsilon\right)^{2}\rfloor.

Note this implies
T\leq C(\mathbf{w}_{0})\cdot\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\log 1/\varepsilon\right)^{2}\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\log 1/\varepsilon\right)^{2}
.

Note as
\varepsilon\leq 1/e
, if
\beta\geq d\sqrt{\log(10)/C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}
and
\varepsilon\leq\sqrt{C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}
, then
T\geq 1
.

Also note in all cases that
\eta\leq\min(1,r(\mathbf{w}_{0}))
, since
\varepsilon\leq 1
,
\beta\geq d
.

Proof.
Define
\mathfrak{F}_{t}
by the natural filtration with respect to
\mathbf{w}_{j},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{j}
for all
j\leq t
. Let

\tau:=\min\{T,\inf\{t:\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)>\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)\}

where
\kappa^{\prime}
comes from
Assumption
3.2
.

Define a stochastic process
Y_{t}
by

Y_{t}:=\begin{cases}\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)+\sum_{j=0}^{t-1}\left(\eta F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{j})-R(\mathbf{w}_{j},\Phi,\eta,\beta,d)\right)&\text{ if }{}t\leq\tau\\
Y_{\tau}&\text{ otherwise if }{}t>\tau\end{cases}

where

R(\mathbf{w}_{j},\Phi,\eta,\beta,d):=\frac{1}{2}\eta^{2}\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{2}+\frac{2C}{3}\eta^{3}\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{3}+2C(\eta d/\beta)^{3/2}.

We show properties of
Y_{t}
in
Lemma
8.1
and
Lemma
8.2
for the sake of presentation. Together, these prove that with probability at
1-\delta
, we have

Y_{t}-Y_{0}\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}C(\eta,t,d,\beta)^{2}\right)\log(T/\delta)}

for all
1\leq t\leq T
, where

C(\eta,t,d,\beta)=4\sqrt{\theta\left(\rho_{\Phi}^{-1}\left(\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)\right)\right)}\cdot\left\|-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{\frac{2\eta}{\beta}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|+4\left\|-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{\frac{2\eta}{\beta}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|^{2}.

Denote the event from
Lemma
8.2
with
\delta=0.1
by
E_{1}
, which occurs with probability at least
0.9
. We claim that if
E_{1}
occurs, then for all
0\leq t\leq T-1
we have
\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\leq\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)
. This clearly finishes the proof.

Suppose for the sake of contradiction that conditioned on
E_{1}
, there exists
0\leq t\leq T-1
where
\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)>\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)
. Hence we have
\tau<T
and for that
\tau
,
\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{\tau})\right)>\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)
. First note if
T=0
this is not possible, so suppose
T\geq 1
from now on. Thus
\log(10T)>1
.

Then by
Assumption
3.2
, for all
t<\tau
we have
\mathbf{w}_{t}\in\mathbb{B}(\vec{\mathbf{0}},R_{1}^{\prime})
. Hence for all
t<\tau
we have by
Assumption
2.2
that

\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|=\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})-\nabla F(\mathbf{w}^{\star})\right\|\leq L\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}-\mathbf{w}^{\star}\right\|^{s}\leq L\left(R_{1}+\left\|\mathbf{w}^{\star}\right\|\right)^{s}=B_{\textsc{grad}}.

We assumed without loss of generality that
L,R_{1}\geq 1
, thus the above upper bound
B_{\textsc{grad}}
is at least 1.

Lemma
8.2
gives us a way to upper bound
Y_{\tau}-Y_{0}
(since we condition on
E_{1}
), so now let’s derive a lower bound on
Y_{\tau}-Y_{0}
. We will then show that these upper and lower bounds are contradictory to complete the proof.

By definition of
\tau
and as
\rho_{\Phi},\theta
are increasing, we have
\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{\tau})\right)>\theta\left(\rho_{\Phi}^{-1}\left(\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)\right)\right)
. Moreover, by the above and as
B_{\textsc{grad}}\geq 1
, we have for all
t<\tau
that

R(\mathbf{w}_{j},\Phi,\eta,\beta,d)\leq\frac{1}{2}\eta^{2}B_{\textsc{grad}}^{3}+\frac{2C}{3}\eta^{3}B_{\textsc{grad}}^{3}+2C(\eta d/\beta)^{3/2}.

Thus as
Y_{0}=\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)
, we have

\displaystyle Y_{\tau}-Y_{0}

\displaystyle=\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{\tau})\right)+\sum_{j=0}^{\tau-1}\left(\eta F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{j})-R(\mathbf{w}_{j},\Phi,\eta,\beta,d)\right)-\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)

\displaystyle\geq\theta\left(\rho_{\Phi}^{-1}\left(\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)\right)\right)-\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)-\left(\left(\frac{1}{2}\eta^{2}+\frac{2C}{3}\eta^{3}\right)B_{\textsc{grad}}^{3}+2C(\eta d/\beta)^{3/2}\right)\tau

\displaystyle\geq\theta\left(\rho_{\Phi}^{-1}\left(\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)\right)\right)-\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)-\left(\frac{3}{2}CB_{\textsc{grad}}^{3}\eta^{2}+2C(\eta d/\beta)^{3/2}\right)T.

(18)

Here the first inequality crucially uses the definition of
\tau
itself. The second inequality follows from
\eta\leq\frac{3}{4C}
. Note as
\theta
and
\rho_{\Phi}
are increasing and as
\kappa^{\prime}>1
,
A_{0}(\mathbf{w}_{0})=\theta\left(\rho_{\Phi}^{-1}\left(\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)\right)\right)-\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)>0
.

Now recall that as we condition on
E_{1}
from
Lemma
8.2
, we have that

Y_{\tau}-Y_{0}\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}C(\eta,t,d,\beta)^{2}\right)\log(10T)}

since the above holds for every
1\leq t\leq T
, and thus holds for every value
\tau
could take.
Recall
\left\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|=\sqrt{d}
always holds. Therefore, we obtain via Triangle Inequality and Young’s Inequality that for all
t<\tau
,

\displaystyle C(\eta,t,d,\beta)

\displaystyle\leq 4\left\|-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{\frac{2\eta}{\beta}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|+4\left\|-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{\frac{2\eta}{\beta}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 4\left(B_{\textsc{grad}}\eta+2\sqrt{\eta d/\beta}\right)+8\eta^{2}B_{\textsc{grad}}^{2}+16\eta d/\beta.

Here the first inequality follows by definition of
\theta
in
Lemma
6.1
, we can easily check
\theta\leq 1
assuming
\rho_{\Phi}
is scaled and shifted appropriately by an absolute constant.

Observe that as
\eta\leq 1
, we have
\eta d/\beta\leq 1
as
\beta\geq d
. Since in all cases we chose
\eta\leq\min\left(1,\frac{1}{B_{\textsc{grad}}}\right)
, we have for all
t<\tau
that

\displaystyle C(\eta,t,d,\beta)

\displaystyle\leq 24\left(B_{\textsc{grad}}\eta+\sqrt{\eta d/\beta}\right).

This implies

\displaystyle Y_{\tau}-Y_{0}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}C(\eta,t,d,\beta)^{2}\right)\log(10T)}

\displaystyle\leq 12\sqrt{2}\left(B_{\textsc{grad}}\eta+\sqrt{\eta d/\beta}\right)\sqrt{T\log\left(10T\right)}.

(19)

Putting together these lower and upper bounds
(
18
)
and
(
19
)
, obtain

\displaystyle\theta\left(\rho_{\Phi}^{-1}\left(\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)\right)\right)-\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)-\left(\frac{3}{2}CB_{\textsc{grad}}^{3}\eta^{2}+2C(\eta d/\beta)^{3/2}\right)T

\displaystyle\leq Y_{\tau}-Y_{0}

\displaystyle\leq 12\sqrt{2}\left(B_{\textsc{grad}}\eta+\sqrt{\eta d/\beta}\right)\sqrt{T\log\left(10T\right)}.

That is, recalling the definition of
A_{0}(\mathbf{w}_{0})
,

\displaystyle 0<A_{0}(\mathbf{w}_{0})

\displaystyle=\theta\left(\rho_{\Phi}^{-1}\left(\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)\right)\right)-\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{3}{2}CB_{\textsc{grad}}^{3}\eta^{2}+2C(\eta d/\beta)^{3/2}\right)T+12\sqrt{2}\left(B_{\textsc{grad}}\eta+\sqrt{\eta d/\beta}\right)\sqrt{T\log\left(10T\right)}.

(20)

Noting the left hand side is a positive constant, we aim to show with our choice of
\eta
and
T
that this gives contradiction. Break into our original cases:

1.

If
\beta\leq\frac{d}{\varepsilon/\left(\log 1/\varepsilon\right)^{2}}
: Recall
r(\mathbf{w}_{0})\leq 1
. By our choice of
\eta=r(\mathbf{w}_{0})\frac{d}{\beta}\leq 1
, we have

\left(\eta d/\beta\right)^{1/2}\leq\frac{(\eta^{2})^{1/2}}{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})^{1/2}}=\frac{\eta}{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})^{1/2}},\left(\eta d/\beta\right)^{3/2}=\frac{(\eta^{2})^{3/2}}{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})^{3/2}}\leq\frac{\eta^{2}}{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})^{3/2}}.

Now, using this we note the right hand side of
(
20
)
is at most

\displaystyle A_{1}(\mathbf{w}_{0})\left(\left(\eta^{2}+\frac{\eta^{2}}{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})^{3/2}}\right)T+\left(\eta+\frac{\eta}{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})^{1/2}}\right)\sqrt{T\log(10T)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{A_{1}(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})^{3/2}}\left(2\eta^{2}T+2\eta\sqrt{T\log(10T)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4A_{1}(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})^{3/2}}\cdot\eta\sqrt{T\log(10T)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4A_{1}(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})^{3/2}}\cdot r(\mathbf{w}_{0})\cdot\frac{d}{\beta}\cdot\frac{A_{0}(\mathbf{w}_{0})\cdot r(\mathbf{w}_{0})^{1/2}}{8A_{1}(\mathbf{w}_{0})}\cdot\frac{\beta}{d}

\displaystyle<\frac{A_{0}(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{2}.

The first inequality is because
\eta\leq 1
and definition of
A_{1}(\mathbf{w}_{0})
, and because
r(\mathbf{w}_{0})\leq 1
. The second inequality is because
\eta\sqrt{T}\leq\frac{d}{\beta}\cdot\frac{\beta}{d}=1
(recall
T\geq 1
else we are done). The third inequality follows recalling the definitions of
\eta
and
T
in terms of
A_{0}(\mathbf{w}_{0})
,
A_{1}(\mathbf{w}_{0})
,
r(\mathbf{w}_{0})
and
C(\mathbf{w}_{0})
(note
z\log(10z)
is increasing on
z\geq 1
). The last inequality follows from definition of
C(\mathbf{w}_{0})
.

As
A_{0}(\mathbf{w}_{0})>0
, this contradicts
(
20
)
which is exactly what we want.

2.

If
\beta\geq\frac{d}{\varepsilon/\left(\log 1/\varepsilon\right)^{2}}
: The strategy is similar. This time, we have by the condition that

\frac{d}{\beta}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{\left(\log 1/\varepsilon\right)^{2}}=\frac{\eta}{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})}

hence

\left(\frac{\eta d}{\beta}\right)^{1/2}\leq\frac{\eta}{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})^{1/2}}.

Thus, we have the right hand side of
(
20
)
is at most

\displaystyle A_{1}(\mathbf{w}_{0})\left(\left(\eta^{2}+\frac{\eta^{2}}{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})^{3/2}}\right)T+\left(\eta+\frac{\eta}{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})^{1/2}}\right)\sqrt{T\log(10T)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{A_{1}(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})^{3/2}}\left(2\eta^{2}T+2\eta\sqrt{T\log(10T)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4A_{1}(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})^{3/2}}\cdot\eta\sqrt{T\log(10T)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4A_{1}(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})^{3/2}}\cdot r(\mathbf{w}_{0})\cdot\frac{\varepsilon}{(\log 1/\varepsilon)^{2}}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}}{\varepsilon}\cdot\log(1/\varepsilon)\cdot\sqrt{\log\left(\frac{10}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\log(1/\varepsilon)^{2}\right)\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{A_{0}(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{2}\sqrt{\frac{\log(\frac{\sqrt{10}}{\varepsilon}(\log 1/\varepsilon))}{\log^{2}(1/\varepsilon)}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{A_{0}(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{2\sqrt{2}}.

The first inequality is because
\eta\leq 1
, the definition of
A_{1}(\mathbf{w}_{0})
, and because
r(\mathbf{w}_{0})\leq 1
. The second inequality is because
\varepsilon\leq 1/e
,
T\geq 1
and so
\eta\sqrt{T}\leq\frac{1}{\log\left(1/\varepsilon\right)}\leq 1
. The third inequality is by definition of
\eta
and
T
and as
C(\mathbf{w}_{0})\leq 1
,
T\geq 1
(note
z\log(10z)
is increasing on
z\geq 1
). The fourth inequality is by definition of
C(\mathbf{w}_{0})
. The last inequality is by definition of
\varepsilon
and
C^{\prime}
. In detail, since
\frac{\log(\sqrt{10}z(\log z))}{\log^{2}(z)}
is continuous, decreasing for large enough
z
, and
\lim_{z\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log(\sqrt{10}z(\log z))}{\log^{2}(z)}=0
, let
z:=\frac{1}{C^{\prime}}
be the largest solution to
\frac{\log(\sqrt{10}z(\log z))}{\log^{2}(z)}=1/2
. Thus, as
\varepsilon<C^{\prime}
we have the last inequality.

This contradicts
(
20
)
as
A_{0}(\mathbf{w}_{0})>0
, which again is exactly what we want.

In all cases we obtain a contradiction conditioned on
E_{1}
, which occurs with probability at least 0.9 from the earlier discussion. Hence with probability at least 0.9 we have
\rho_{\Phi}(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t}))\leq\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0}))
for all
0\leq t\leq T-1
as desired.
∎

Now, with these parts in hand, we can prove
Theorem
6.1
.

Proof.
First, it clearly suffices to prove for small enough
\varepsilon
, in particular when
\varepsilon\leq\min\left(1/e,C^{\prime},\sqrt{\frac{C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{\log 10}}\right)
. By the logic in
Lemma
6.3
, based on our cases on
\beta
and
\varepsilon
, the
T
that we choose will always be at least 1. This is as
\varepsilon\leq 1/e
, so our cutoff of
\min\left(1/e,C^{\prime},\sqrt{\frac{C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{\log 10}}\right)
for
\varepsilon
guarantees that the
\varepsilon
we use is upper bounded by
\sqrt{C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}
.
Therefore suppose
\varepsilon\leq\min\left(1/e,C^{\prime},\sqrt{\frac{C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{\log 10}}\right)
. In this case,
\varepsilon\leq 1
, so we can also assume
\eta\leq 1
by the choice of
\eta
given in
Lemma
6.3
.

Let
E_{1}
be the event that
\rho_{\Phi}(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t}))\leq\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0}))
for all
1\leq t\leq T
. From
Lemma
6.3
, we know
E_{1}
holds with probability at least 0.9 for the choice of
\eta,T
given there. By
Assumption
3.2
, this means that conditioned on
E_{1}
, all the
\mathbf{w}_{t}\in\mathbb{B}(\vec{\mathbf{0}},R_{1})
for
1\leq t\leq T
. By the same derivation as
Lemma
6.3
, this means

\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|\leq B_{\textsc{grad}}\text{ for all }{}1\leq t\leq T.\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:gradupperboundoracle}

As
\theta^{\prime}\geq 0
by
Lemma
6.1
,
\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{t})\theta^{\prime}(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t}))\geq 0
, thus we see by Markov’s Inequality that with probability at least 0.9,

\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{t})\theta^{\prime}(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t}))\leq 10\mathbb{E}[\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{t})\theta^{\prime}(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t}))].\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:discretizationoraclemarkov}

Let
E_{2}
be the event that this above inequality holds.

Finally, consider
\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{r}\right]
for
r\in\{2,3\}
. Note by constructing a martingale from partial sums of the sequence minus the expectation of the partial sum, we can show that with probability at least 0.975 for a given
r\in\{2,3\}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{r}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{r}\right]-2\sqrt{2}\max_{0\leq t\leq T-1}\left(\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{r}\right)\cdot\sqrt{T\log 40}.

(23)

Let
E_{3}
be the intersection of these two events for
r\in\{2,3\}
, so
E_{3}
has probability at least 0.95.

The last step we need is the following: summing and telescoping the result from
Lemma
6.2
, and using that
\theta\left(\Phi(z)\right)\geq 0
, we obtain

\displaystyle\eta\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{t})\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)+2C(\eta d/\beta)^{3/2}T

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\eta^{2}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right]+\frac{2C}{3}\eta^{3}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{3}\right].

(24)

Here, we took full expectations over the noise sequence
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}
in the above.

Let

\displaystyle M_{1}(\mathbf{w}_{0})

\displaystyle=10\max\left(\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right),6CB_{\textsc{grad}}^{3}\right),

which is just a
\mathbf{w}_{0}
-dependent constant.

Now we put the above steps together and do a Union Bound over
E_{1},E_{2},E_{3}
. Let
E=E_{1}\cap E_{2}\cap E_{3}
; we have that
E
occurs with probability at least 0.75. Then conditioned on
E
, we see combining
(
6.1
)
,
(
6.1
)
,
(
23
)
, and
(
24
)
that

\displaystyle\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{t})\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)

\displaystyle\leq 10\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{t})\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)}{\eta}+2C(d/\beta)^{3/2}\eta^{1/2}T+\frac{1}{2}\eta\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right]+\frac{2C}{3}\eta^{2}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{3}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)}{\eta}+2C(d/\beta)^{3/2}\eta^{1/2}T

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\frac{1}{2}\eta\left(\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{2}+2\sqrt{2}\max_{0\leq t\leq T-1}\left(\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right)\cdot\sqrt{T\log 40}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\frac{2C}{3}\eta^{2}\left(\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{3}+2\sqrt{2}\max_{0\leq t\leq T-1}\left(\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{3}\right)\cdot\sqrt{T\log 40}\right)

\displaystyle\leq M_{1}(\mathbf{w}_{0})\left(\frac{1}{\eta}+(d/\beta)^{3/2}\eta^{1/2}T+\eta T+\eta^{2}T+\sqrt{T}\right).

The last inequality uses
(
6.1
)
,
\eta\leq 1
, and
B_{\textsc{grad}}\geq 1
.

Note by definition from
Lemma
6.1
we know
\theta^{\prime}>0
always holds. Moreover, because
\theta^{\prime\prime}<0
from
Lemma
6.1
, conditioned on
E
we have

\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\geq\theta^{\prime}\left(\rho_{\Phi}^{-1}\left(\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)\right)\right)\text{ for all }{}0\leq t\leq T-1.

Thus, defining

M(\mathbf{w}_{0})=\frac{M_{1}(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{\theta^{\prime}\left(\rho_{\Phi}^{-1}\left(\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)\right)\right)}\in(0,\infty),

we see that conditioned on
E
which occurs with probability at least 0.75 we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{t})

\displaystyle\leq M(\mathbf{w}_{0})\left(\frac{1}{\eta T}+(d/\beta)^{3/2}\eta^{1/2}+\eta+\eta^{2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq M(\mathbf{w}_{0})\left(\frac{1}{\eta T}+\frac{\eta}{2}+\frac{(d/\beta)^{3}}{2}+2\eta+\frac{1}{2\eta T}+\frac{\eta}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 3M(\mathbf{w}_{0})\left(\frac{1}{\eta T}+\eta+d/\beta\right).

Here we used
\eta\leq 1
,
d/\beta\leq 1
, and AM-GM. We break into cases based on how we set
\eta,T
from
Lemma
6.3
:

1.

If
\beta\leq\frac{d}{\varepsilon/\left(\log 1/\varepsilon\right)^{2}}
: Now recall we set
\eta=r(\mathbf{w}_{0})\frac{d}{\beta}\leq d/\beta
, and that we let
T
be the floor of the unique solution to the equation

z\log(10z)=C(\mathbf{w}_{0})\frac{\beta^{2}}{d^{2}},

where
C(\mathbf{w}_{0})
is defined according to
Lemma
6.3
. Recall we had
T\geq 1
as well as

T\leq C(\mathbf{w}_{0})\frac{\beta^{2}}{d^{2}}\leq\frac{\beta^{2}}{d^{2}}.

Since
T\geq 1
, and as
z\log(10z)
is increasing for
z\geq 1
, it follows via definition of
T
(note
2\lfloor z\rfloor\geq z
for all
z\geq 1
) that

2T\log(20T)\geq C(\mathbf{w}_{0})\frac{\beta^{2}}{d^{2}},

hence

\eta T\geq\frac{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{2\log(20T)}\frac{\beta}{d}.

Thus, we have with probability at least 0.75 that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{t})

\displaystyle\leq 3M(\mathbf{w}_{0})\left(\frac{2\log(20T)}{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}+2\right)\frac{d}{\beta}

\displaystyle\leq 6M(\mathbf{w}_{0})\left(\frac{\log 20+2\log(\beta/d)}{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}+1\right)\frac{d}{\beta}.

That is, we obtain
\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{d}{\beta}\right)
suboptimality with at most
\frac{\beta^{2}}{d^{2}}
iterations.

2.

If
\beta\geq\frac{d}{\varepsilon/\left(\log 1/\varepsilon\right)^{2}}
: Recalling how we set
\eta
and the definition of this case, as well as
\varepsilon\leq 1/e
, we have
\eta,d/\beta\leq\varepsilon
. Moreover, note
\eta T\geq\frac{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{2\varepsilon}
;
T\geq 1
, and
\lfloor z\rfloor\geq\frac{z}{2}
for all
z\geq 1
. Hence, we obtain with probability at least 0.75 that

\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{t})\leq 3M(\mathbf{w}_{0})\left(\frac{2\varepsilon}{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}+2\varepsilon\right)=6M(\mathbf{w}_{0})\left(\frac{1}{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}+1\right)\varepsilon.

That is, we obtain
O(\varepsilon)
suboptimality with at most
T\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\log 1/\varepsilon\right)^{2}
iterations.

∎

6.2
Proof of
Theorem
3.3

In the stochastic gradient oracle setting, we have the following result for optimization of
F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w})
. The proof is very similar to the above. Again note this generalizes to cost function
A(\mathbf{w})
the same way as noted in
Remark
6.1
.

The following is our formal statement of
Theorem
3.3
.

Theorem 6.2
.

Consider
F
and suppose
F
is differentiable. Suppose that we have
(
3.1
)
:

\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}),\nabla F(\mathbf{w})\right\rangle\geq F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w})+\frac{1}{\beta}\Delta\Phi(\mathbf{w}),

for some
\beta>0
. Suppose
Assumption
2.2
,
3.1
, and
3.2
hold. Moreover, suppose we have an unbiased stochastic gradient oracle
\nabla f
that satisfies
Assumption
2.4
, and that
\beta\geq d\sqrt{\frac{\log^{7}(20)}{C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}}
where
C(\cdot)
is defined below.

Define the following quantities:

A_{0}(\mathbf{w}_{0})=\theta\left(\rho_{\Phi}^{-1}\left(\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)\right)\right)-\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)>0,A_{1}(\mathbf{w}_{0})=12\sqrt{2}CB_{\textsc{grad}}^{3}.

Here
C
comes from
Lemma
6.2
and
B_{\textsc{grad}}:=2\left(L\left(R_{1}+\left\|\mathbf{w}^{\star}\right\|\right)^{s}+\sigma_{F}\right)
,
\sigma_{F}
comes from
Assumption
2.4
,
L
comes from
Assumption
2.2
and
R_{1}
coming from
Assumption
3.2
. (If necessary take
C\leftarrow\max(C,1)
,
B_{\textsc{grad}}\leftarrow\max(B_{\textsc{grad}},1)
,
\sigma_{F}\leftarrow\max(\sigma_{F},1)
.) In terms of these define (where
\theta
comes from
Lemma
6.1
),

r(\mathbf{w}_{0})=\min\left(1,\frac{3}{4C},\frac{1}{B_{\textsc{grad}}}\right),C(\mathbf{w}_{0})=\min\left(1,\frac{A_{0}(\mathbf{w}_{0})^{2}r(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{128A_{1}(\mathbf{w}_{0})^{2}}\right).

M(\mathbf{w}_{0})=\frac{10\max\left(\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right),6CB_{\textsc{grad}}^{3}\right)}{\theta^{\prime}\left(\rho_{\Phi}^{-1}\left(\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)\right)\right)}\in(0,\infty).

Consider running
Algorithm
1
except:

•

Each instance of
\log 10
is replaced here with
\log^{7}(20)
.

•

Instead of using the exact gradient
\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})
, we use the stochastic gradient oracle
\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})
.

•

We use
\eta
given in
Lemma
6.6
(rather than from
Lemma
6.3
) and run this process with step size
\eta
for
T
steps also given in
Lemma
6.6
.

Then our algorithm has the following guarantees. First, its runtime
T
is as follows:

1.

If
\varepsilon\leq\min\left(1/e,C^{\prime},\sqrt{\frac{C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{\log^{7}(20)}}\right)
: then

T\leq\begin{cases}\frac{\beta^{2}}{d^{2}}&\text{ if }{}\beta\leq\frac{d}{\varepsilon/\left(\log 1/\varepsilon\right)^{5}}\\
\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\log 1/\varepsilon\right)^{2}&\text{ if }{}\beta\geq\frac{d}{\varepsilon/\left(\log 1/\varepsilon\right)^{5}}.\end{cases}

2.

If
\varepsilon>\min\left(1/e,C^{\prime},\sqrt{\frac{C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{\log^{7}(20)}}\right)
: then we have the same runtime guarantee as implied by above with
\min\left(1/e,C^{\prime},\sqrt{\frac{C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{\log^{7}(20)}}\right)
in place of
\varepsilon
.

In terms of error, we have with probability at least 0.75 (where probability is taken over
\{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t},\mathbf{z}_{t}\}_{0\leq t\leq T-1}
) that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{t})\leq\begin{cases}6M(\mathbf{w}_{0})\left(\frac{2^{7}\log^{7}(40)+2^{14}\log^{7}(\beta/d)}{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}+1\right)\frac{d}{\beta}&\text{ if }{}\beta\leq\frac{d}{\varepsilon/\left(\log 1/\varepsilon\right)^{5}}\\
6M(\mathbf{w}_{0})\left(\frac{1}{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}+1\right)\varepsilon\left(\log 1/\varepsilon\right)^{3}&\text{ if }{}\beta\geq\frac{d}{\varepsilon/\left(\log 1/\varepsilon\right)^{5}}.\end{cases}

Note now that logarithmic boosting tricks proves that a given one of these guarantees can occur with probability at least
1-\delta
using at most
T\log(1/\delta)
steps.

We prove
Theorem
6.2
with a very similar strategy to the proof of
Theorem
6.1
.

The first step is to control the error of the gradient estimates to adapt to the stochastic gradient setting; we also use these results to prove
Theorem
7.1
.

Lemma 6.4
.

Suppose
Assumption
2.4
holds. Letting
\{\mathbf{w}_{t}\}_{0\leq t\leq T-1}
be the sequence of iterates generated by any of the variants of
SGLD
used in our algorithms on
F
, using stochastic gradient estimates based on
\{\mathbf{z}_{t}\}_{0\leq t\leq T-1}
. Then we have

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{z}_{t}}\left[\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})-\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right]\leq\sigma_{F}^{2},

and moreover

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{z}_{t}}\left[\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right]\leq 2\sigma_{F}^{2}+2\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{2},\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{z}_{t}}\left[\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\|^{3}\right]\leq 8\sigma_{F}^{3}+4\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{3}.

Also with probability at least
1-\delta
we have

\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\|\leq\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|+\sigma_{F}\sqrt{\log\left(T/\delta\right)}\text{ for all }{}0\leq t\leq T-1.

Here, all probabilities and expectations are taken over the
\mathbf{z}_{t}
.

Proof.
Clearly
\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})-\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{2}
is non-negative, therefore

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})-\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{t=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})-\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{2}\geq t\right)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{t=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})-\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|\geq\sqrt{t}\right)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{t=0}^{\infty}e^{-t/\sigma_{F}^{2}}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\sigma_{F}^{2}.

Now by Young’s Inequality we have pointwise

\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\|^{2}\leq 2\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})-\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{2}+2\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{2},

and combining with the above gives

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right]\leq 2\sigma_{F}^{2}+2\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right].

Analogously, note

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})-\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{3}\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{t=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})-\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{3}\geq t\right)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{t=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})-\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|\geq t^{1/3}\right)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{t=0}^{\infty}e^{-t^{2/3}/\sigma_{F}^{2}}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq 2\sigma_{F}^{3}.

The inequality
\left\|\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{b}\right\|^{3}\leq 4\left\|\mathbf{a}\right\|^{3}+4\left\|\mathbf{b}\right\|^{3}
thus yields

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\|^{3}\right]\leq 8\sigma_{F}^{3}+4\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{3}\right].

For a high probability statement, note for any
0\leq t\leq T-1
, we have
\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})-\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|\geq\sigma_{F}\sqrt{\log\left(T/\delta\right)}
with probability at most
\delta/T
. A Union Bound and Triangle Inequality implies that with probability at least
1-\delta
we have

\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\|\leq\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|+\sigma_{F}\sqrt{\log\left(T/\delta\right)}\text{ for all }{}0\leq t\leq T-1.

∎

Now analogously as before with
Lemma
6.2
, we prove a one-step discretization result. The main difference now is that we have to do the argument in a way that handles the stochasticity of the gradient estimates, but the same idea goes through.

Lemma 6.5
.

For one iteration of
SGLD
starting at arbitrary
\mathbf{w}_{t}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t},\mathbf{z}_{t}}\left[\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)-\eta\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{t})

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\eta^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{z}_{t}}\left[\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right]+\frac{2C}{3}\eta^{3}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{z}_{t}}\left[\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\|^{3}\right]+2C(\eta d/\beta)^{3/2}

where
p
and
C
are defined from
Lemma
6.1
.

Proof.
First, apply
Lemma
6.1
with
\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{w}_{t}
and
\mathbf{u}=-\eta\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}
to obtain

\displaystyle\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right)

\displaystyle=\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t}-\eta\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)+\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t}),-\eta\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\rangle

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\left\langle\nabla^{2}\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\left(-\eta\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right),-\eta\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\frac{C}{6}\left\|-\eta\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|^{3}

where
C
is defined in the proof of
Lemma
6.1
.

We take expectations of this inequality with respect to
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}
and
\mathbf{z}_{t}
. Let’s consider what each term of the upper bound becomes when we take expectations.

•

First order term: Since
\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})
is unbiased,
\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t},\mathbf{z}_{t}}\left[\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right]=\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})
. Thus as
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}
has mean of the 0 vector,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t},\mathbf{z}_{t}}\left[\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t}),-\eta\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\rangle\right]
