\displaystyle=\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\left(\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t}),-\eta\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t},\mathbf{z}_{t}}\left[\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right]\right\rangle+\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t}),\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t},\mathbf{z}_{t}}\left[\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right]\right\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=-\eta\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t}),\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\rangle.

•

Second order term: Note

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t},\mathbf{z}_{t}}\left[\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\left\langle\nabla^{2}\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\left(-\eta\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right),-\eta\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\rangle\right]

\displaystyle=\eta^{2}\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t},\mathbf{z}_{t}}\left[\left\langle\nabla^{2}\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t}),\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\rangle\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt-2\eta\left(2\eta/\beta\right)^{1/2}\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\left\langle\nabla^{2}\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}}\left[\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right],\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{z}_{t}}\left[\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right]\right\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\left(2\eta/\beta\right)\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t},\mathbf{z}_{t}}\left[\left\langle\nabla^{2}\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\rangle\right]

\displaystyle=\eta^{2}\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{z}_{t}}\left[\left\langle\nabla^{2}\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t}),\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\rangle\right]+\left(2\eta/\beta\right)\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\Delta\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t}).

Here we used that
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}
has zero mean as a vector and that
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t},\mathbf{z}_{t}
are clearly independent to compute the cross term.
The calculation of

\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t},\mathbf{z}_{t}}\left[\left\langle\nabla^{2}\Phi(w_{t})\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\rangle\right]=\Delta\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})

is the same as before. Note this expectation has no
\mathbf{z}_{t}
dependence.

•

Third order term: Again we use for all
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\left\|\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{b}\right\|^{3}\leq 4\left\|\mathbf{a}\right\|^{3}+4\|\mathbf{b}\|^{3}.

Using this inequality pointwise we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t},\mathbf{z}_{t}}\left[\left\|-\eta\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|^{3}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 4\eta^{3}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{z}_{t}}\left[\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\|^{3}\right]+4\left(2\eta/\beta\right)^{3/2}d^{3/2}.

The last step is because
\left\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|=\sqrt{d}
always holds deterministically.

We put this together, noting
\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\geq 0
from
Lemma
6.1
which means we can use the admissability condition
(
3.1
)
which we use to upper bound the first order term. This gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t},\mathbf{z}_{t}}\left[\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)-\eta\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t}),\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\rangle

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\left(\eta^{2}\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{z}_{t}}\left[\left\langle\nabla^{2}\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t}),\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\rangle\right]+\left(2\eta/\beta\right)\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\Delta\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\frac{C}{6}\left(4\eta^{3}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{z}_{t}}\left[\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\|^{3}\right]+4\left(2\eta/\beta\right)^{3/2}d^{3/2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)-\eta\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\left(F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\frac{1}{\beta}\Delta\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\left(\eta^{2}\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\left\|\nabla^{2}\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|_{\mathrm{op}{}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{z}_{t}}\left[\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right]+\left(2\eta/\beta\right)\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\Delta\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\frac{C}{6}\left(4\eta^{3}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{z}_{t}}\left[\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\|^{3}\right]+4\left(2\eta/\beta\right)^{3/2}d^{3/2}\right).

The second inequality follows analogously as in the proof of
Lemma
6.2
; pointwise we have

\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})^{T}\nabla^{2}\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\leq\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\|^{2}||\nabla^{2}\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})||_{\mathrm{op}{}},

and the fact that

\theta^{\prime}(z)\leq\frac{1}{\rho_{\Phi,2}(z)}

always holds. Also note the terms
\eta\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\cdot\frac{1}{\beta}\Delta\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})
and
\frac{1}{2}\left(2\eta/\beta\right)\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\Delta\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})
cancel out. Thus our above bound becomes

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t},\mathbf{z}_{t}}\left[\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)-\eta\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{t})

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\eta^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{z}_{t}}\left[\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right]+\frac{2C}{3}\eta^{3}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{z}_{t}}\left[\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\|^{3}\right]+2C\left(\eta/\beta\right)^{3/2}d^{3/2}.

∎

Next, analogously as
Lemma
6.3
, we prove that
\Phi
indeed is a potential.

Lemma 6.6
.

Suppose
\beta\geq d
and
\varepsilon<1/e
. Additionally suppose
\varepsilon<C^{\prime}
where
z=\frac{1}{C^{\prime}}
is the largest solution to
\frac{\log^{7}(\sqrt{20}z(\log z))}{\log^{8}(z)}=1/2
(the existence of such a
C^{\prime}>0
is obvious).

With probability at least
0.9
, we have that

\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\leq\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right),

for all
0\leq t\leq T-1
if
T,\eta
are chosen as follows, where
\kappa^{\prime}
comes from
Assumption
3.2
. First define

A_{0}(\mathbf{w}_{0})=\theta\left(\rho_{\Phi}^{-1}\left(\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)\right)\right)-\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)>0,A_{1}(\mathbf{w}_{0})=12\sqrt{2}CB_{\textsc{grad}}^{3}.

Here
C
comes from
Lemma
6.2
and
B_{\textsc{grad}}:=2\left(L\left(R_{1}+\left\|\mathbf{w}^{\star}\right\|\right)^{s}+\sigma_{F}\right)
,
\sigma_{F}
comes from
Assumption
2.4
,
L
comes from
Assumption
2.2
and
R_{1}
comes from
Assumption
3.2
. (Again if necessary take
C\leftarrow\max(C,1)
,
B_{\textsc{grad}}\leftarrow\max(B_{\textsc{grad}},1)
,
\sigma_{F}\leftarrow\max(\sigma_{F},1)
.)

Also define

r(\mathbf{w}_{0})=\min\left(1,\frac{3}{4C},\frac{1}{B_{\textsc{grad}}}\right),C(\mathbf{w}_{0})=\min\left(1,\frac{A_{0}(\mathbf{w}_{0})^{2}r(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{128A_{1}(\mathbf{w}_{0})^{2}}\right).

Now we choose
T,\eta
based on cases:

1.

If
\beta\leq\frac{d}{\varepsilon/\left(\log 1/\varepsilon\right)^{5}}
: Take
T,\eta
such that

\eta=r(\mathbf{w}_{0})\frac{d}{\beta},

and
T
is the unique solution to the equation

z\log^{7}(20z)=C(\mathbf{w}_{0})\frac{\beta^{2}}{d^{2}}.

Existence and uniqueness is clear since
z\log^{7}(20z)
is surjective on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and for every positive real
t
, exactly one positive real
z
is such that
z\log^{7}(20z)=t
. By the same argument as in
Lemma
6.3
, this means
T\leq\frac{\beta^{2}}{d^{2}}
. Also note this means

\eta\leq\min\left(1,\frac{3}{4C},\frac{1}{B_{\textsc{grad}}},\frac{d}{\beta}\right).

2.

If
\beta\geq\frac{d}{\varepsilon/\left(\log 1/\varepsilon\right)^{5}}
: Take
T,\eta
such that

\eta=r(\mathbf{w}_{0})\cdot\frac{\varepsilon}{\left(\log 1/\varepsilon\right)^{5}},T=\lfloor\frac{C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot\left(\log 1/\varepsilon\right)^{2}\rfloor.

Note this implies
T\leq C(\mathbf{w}_{0})\cdot\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\log 1/\varepsilon\right)^{2}\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\log 1/\varepsilon\right)^{2}
.

Note as
\varepsilon\leq 1/e
, if
\beta\geq d\sqrt{\log^{7}(20)/C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}
and
\varepsilon\leq\sqrt{C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}
, then
T\geq 1
.

Also note in all cases that
\eta\leq\min\left(1,r(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)
, since
\varepsilon\leq 1
,
\beta\geq d
.

Proof.
Define
\mathfrak{F}_{t}
by the natural filtration with respect to
\mathbf{w}_{j},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{j},\mathbf{z}_{j}
for all
j\leq t
. Again, let

\tau:=\min\{T,\inf\{t:\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)>\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)\},

where
\kappa^{\prime}
comes from
Assumption
3.2
.

Again, define a stochastic process
Y_{t}
by

Y_{t}:=\begin{cases}\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)+\sum_{j=0}^{t-1}\left(\eta F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{j})-R(\mathbf{w}_{j},\Phi,\eta,\beta,d)\right)&\text{ if }{}t\leq\tau\\
Y_{\tau}&\text{ otherwise if }{}t>\tau,\end{cases}

where now we have

R(\mathbf{w}_{j},\Phi,\eta,\beta,d):=\frac{1}{2}\eta^{2}\left(2\sigma_{F}^{2}+2\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right)+\frac{2C}{3}\eta^{3}\left(8\sigma_{F}^{3}+4\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{3}\right)+2C(\eta d/\beta)^{3/2}.

Note from
Lemma
6.4
that

R(\mathbf{w}_{j},\Phi,\eta,\beta,d)\geq\frac{1}{2}\eta^{2}\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\|^{2}+\frac{2C}{3}\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\|^{3}+2C(\eta d/\beta)^{3/2}.

The same derivation as in the proofs of
Lemma
8.1
(now using
Lemma
6.5
) and
Lemma
8.2
give the following two results adapted to this setting:

Lemma 6.7
.

Y_{t}
is a supermartingale with respect to
\mathfrak{F}_{t}
.

Lemma 6.8
.

With probability at least
1-\delta
, we have

Y_{t}-Y_{0}\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}C(\eta,t,d,\beta)^{2}\right)\log(T/\delta)}

for all
1\leq t\leq T
, where

C(\eta,t,d,\beta)=4\sqrt{\theta\left(\rho_{\Phi}^{-1}\left(\kappa\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(w_{0})\right)\right)\right)}\cdot\left\|-\eta\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})+\sqrt{\frac{2\eta}{\beta}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|+4\left\|-\eta\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})+\sqrt{\frac{2\eta}{\beta}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|^{2}.

Note we need to have high-probability control over the
\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})
, rather than just control over their moments, to upper bound the
C(\eta,t,d,\beta)
in the above.

Denote the event from
Lemma
6.8
with
\delta=0.05
by
E_{1}^{\prime}
, which occurs with probability at least
0.95
.

Also denote the event from
Lemma
6.4
with
\delta=0.05
by
E_{1}^{\prime\prime}
, which occurs with probability at least
0.95
.

Now define
E_{1}=E_{1}^{\prime}\cap E_{1}^{\prime\prime}
, which occurs with probability at least 0.9. We claim that if
E_{1}
occurs, then for all
0\leq t\leq T-1
we have
\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\leq\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)
. This clearly finishes the proof.

Suppose for the sake of contradiction that there exists
0\leq t\leq T-1
where
\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)>\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)
. Hence we have
\tau<T
and for that
\tau
,
\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{\tau})\right)>\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)
. First note if
T=0
this is not possible, so suppose
T\geq 1
from now on. Thus
\log(20T)>1
.

Then by
Assumption
3.2
, for all
t<\tau
we have
\mathbf{w}_{t}\in\mathbb{B}(\vec{\mathbf{0}},R_{1}^{\prime})
. Hence for all
t<\tau
we have by
Assumption
2.2
that

\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|=\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})-\nabla F(\mathbf{w}^{\star})\right\|\leq L\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}-\mathbf{w}^{\star}\right\|^{s}\leq L\left(R_{1}+\left\|\mathbf{w}^{\star}\right\|\right)^{s}.

Since
E_{1}^{\prime\prime}
holds as we condition on
E_{1}
, by
Lemma
6.4
gives that for all
0\leq t\leq T-1
we have

\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\|\leq\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|+\sigma_{F}\sqrt{\log(20T)}\leq B_{\textsc{grad}}\sqrt{\log(20T)}.\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:upperboundgradhighprob}

We assumed without loss of generality that
L,R_{1}\geq 1
, thus the above upper bound is at least 1. (Note compared to the proof of
Lemma
6.3
that the definition of
B_{\textsc{grad}}
changed.)

Lemma
8.2
gives us a way to upper bound
Y_{\tau}-Y_{0}
(since we condition on
E_{1}
), so now let’s derive a lower bound on
Y_{\tau}-Y_{0}
. We will then show that these upper and lower bounds are contradictory to complete the proof.

Note

\displaystyle R(\mathbf{w}_{j},\Phi,\eta,\beta,d)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\eta^{2}\left(2\sigma_{F}^{2}+2\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right)+\frac{2C}{3}\eta^{3}\left(8\sigma_{F}^{3}+4\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{3}\right)+2C(\eta d/\beta)^{3/2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\eta^{2}B_{\textsc{grad}}^{2}+\frac{2C}{3}\eta^{3}B_{\textsc{grad}}^{3}+2C(\eta d/\beta)^{3/2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3}{2}CB_{\textsc{grad}}^{3}+2C(\eta d/\beta)^{3/2}.

The above uses
\eta\leq 1
, our assumption we made without loss of generality that
C\geq 1
, the definition of
B_{\textsc{grad}}
, and that
B_{\textsc{grad}}\geq 1
.

Now by definition of
\tau
and as
Y_{0}=\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)
, similarly to the proof of
Lemma
6.3
we get

\displaystyle Y_{\tau}-Y_{0}

\displaystyle>\theta\left(\rho_{\Phi}^{-1}\left(\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)\right)\right)-\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)-\left(\frac{3}{2}CB_{\textsc{grad}}^{3}\eta^{2}+2C(\eta d/\beta)^{3/2}\right)T.

(26)

Now we use
Lemma
6.8
to upper bound
Y_{\tau}-Y_{0}
. Again recall
\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\|=\sqrt{d}
always holds, as well as
\theta\leq 1
. Thus via the same derivation as in the proof of
Lemma
6.3
, using
(
6.2
)
, we obtain for all
t<\tau
that

\displaystyle C(\eta,t,d,\beta)

\displaystyle\leq 24\left(B_{\textsc{grad}}\eta+\sqrt{\eta d/\beta}\right)\log^{6}(20T).

This implies

\displaystyle Y_{\tau}-Y_{0}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}C(\eta,t,d,\beta)^{2}\right)\log^{7}(20T)}

\displaystyle\leq 12\sqrt{2}\left(B_{\textsc{grad}}\eta+\sqrt{\eta d/\beta}\right)\sqrt{T\log^{7}\left(20T\right)}.

(27)

Similarly as before, putting together our lower and upper bounds
(
26
)
and
(
27
)
on
Y_{\tau}-Y_{0}
, now we aim to show the following cannot hold:

\displaystyle 0<A_{0}(\mathbf{w}_{0})

\displaystyle=\rho_{\Phi}^{-1}\left(\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)\right)-\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{3}{2}CB_{\textsc{grad}}^{3}\eta^{2}+2C(\eta d/\beta)^{3/2}\right)T+12\sqrt{2}\left(B_{\textsc{grad}}\eta+\sqrt{\eta d/\beta}\right)\sqrt{T\log^{7}\left(20T\right)}.

(28)

Again note the left hand side is a positive constant. We aim to show with our choice of
\eta
and
T
that this gives contradiction. Break into our original cases:

1.

If
\beta\leq\frac{d}{\varepsilon/\left(\log 1/\varepsilon\right)^{5}}
: Once more by our choice of
\eta
, we have
\eta\leq 1,\frac{d}{\beta}=\frac{\eta}{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})}
, thus

\left(\eta d/\beta\right)^{1/2},\left(\eta d/\beta\right)^{3/2}\leq\frac{\eta}{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})^{3/2}}.

Therefore, an analogous derivation as in the proof of
Lemma
6.3
gives that the right hand side of
(
28
)
is at most

\displaystyle\frac{A_{1}(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})^{3/2}}\left(2\eta^{2}T+2\eta\sqrt{T\log^{7}(20T)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4A_{1}(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})^{3/2}}\eta\sqrt{T\log^{7}(20T)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4A_{1}(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})^{3/2}}\cdot r(\mathbf{w}_{0})\cdot\frac{d}{\beta}\cdot\frac{A_{0}(\mathbf{w}_{0})r(\mathbf{w}_{0})^{1/2}}{8A_{1}(\mathbf{w}_{0})}\cdot\frac{\beta}{d}

\displaystyle<\frac{A_{0}(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{2}.

The first inequality follows as
T\geq 1
and
\eta\sqrt{T}\leq 1
. The fourth inequality follows recalling the definitions of
\eta
and
T
in terms of
A_{0}(\mathbf{w}_{0})
,
A_{1}(\mathbf{w}_{0})
,
r(\mathbf{w}_{0})
and
C(\mathbf{w}_{0})
(note
z\log^{7}(20z)
is increasing on
x\geq 1
). The last inequality follows from definition of
C(\mathbf{w}_{0})
. As
A_{0}(\mathbf{w}_{0})>0
, this contradicts
(
28
)
which is exactly what we want.

2.

If
\beta\geq\frac{d}{\varepsilon/\left(\log 1/\varepsilon\right)^{5}}
: The strategy is similar. This time, we have by the condition that

\frac{d}{\beta}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{\left(\log 1/\varepsilon\right)^{5}}=\frac{\eta}{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})},

which implies

\left(\frac{\eta d}{\beta}\right)^{1/2}\leq\frac{\eta}{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})^{1/2}}.

Therefore, an analogous derivation as in the proof of
Lemma
6.3
gives that the right hand side of
(
28
)
is at most

\displaystyle\frac{A_{1}(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})^{3/2}}\left(2\eta^{2}T+2\eta\sqrt{T\log^{7}(20T)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4A_{1}(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})^{3/2}}\eta\sqrt{T\log^{7}(20T)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4A_{1}(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})^{3/2}}\cdot r(\mathbf{w}_{0})\cdot\frac{\varepsilon}{\left(\log 1/\varepsilon\right)^{5}}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}}{\varepsilon}\cdot(\log 1/\varepsilon)\cdot\sqrt{\log^{7}\left(\frac{20}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\log\left(1/\varepsilon\right)^{2}\right)\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{A_{0}(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{2}\sqrt{\frac{\log^{7}\left(\frac{\sqrt{20}}{\varepsilon}\left(\log 1/\varepsilon\right)\right)}{\log^{8}\left(1/\varepsilon\right)}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{A_{0}(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{2\sqrt{2}}.

The first inequality follows as
T\geq 1
,
\varepsilon\leq 1/e
and so
\eta\sqrt{T}\leq 1
. The second inequality is by definition of
\eta
and
T
and as
C(\mathbf{w}_{0})\leq 1
,
T\geq 1
(note
z\log^{7}(20z)
is increasing on
z\geq 1
). The third inequality is by definition of
C(\mathbf{w}_{0})
. The last inequality is by definition of
\varepsilon
and
C^{\prime}
. In detail, since
\frac{\log^{7}(\sqrt{20}z(\log z))}{\log^{8}(z)}
is continuous, decreasing for large enough
z
, and
\lim_{z\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\log^{7}(\sqrt{20}z(\log z))}{\log^{8}(z)}=0
, let
z:=\frac{1}{C^{\prime}}
be the largest solution to
\frac{\log^{7}(\sqrt{20}z(\log z))}{\log^{8}(z)}=1/2
. Thus, as
\varepsilon<C^{\prime}
we have the last inequality. This contradicts
(
28
)
as
A_{0}(\mathbf{w}_{0})>0
, which again is exactly what we want.

In all cases we obtain a contradiction conditioned on
E_{1}
, which occurs with probability at least 0.9 from the earlier discussion. Hence with probability at least 0.9 we have
\rho_{\Phi}(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t}))\leq\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0}))
for all
0\leq t\leq T-1
as desired.
∎

Finally, we can conclude again similarly as the proof of
Theorem
6.1
to prove
Theorem
6.2
.

Proof.
Again note by the logic in
Lemma
6.6
, based on our cases on
\beta
and
\varepsilon
, the
T
that we choose will always be at least 1. Moreover, in the same way as in the proof of
Theorem
6.1
, we can reduce to proving the case when
\varepsilon\leq\min\left(1/e,C^{\prime},\sqrt{\frac{C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{\log^{7}(20)}}\right)
where
C^{\prime}
is defined from
Lemma
6.6
. We also have
\varepsilon,\eta\leq 1
as a consequence.

Let
E_{1}
be the event that
\rho_{\Phi}(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t}))\leq\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0}))
for all
1\leq t\leq T
. From
Lemma
6.6
, we know
E_{1}
holds with probability at least 0.9 for the choice of
\eta,T
given there. By
Assumption
3.2
, this means that conditioned on
E_{1}
, all the
\mathbf{w}_{t}\in\mathbb{B}(\vec{\mathbf{0}},R_{1})
for
1\leq t\leq T
. By the same derivation as
Lemma
6.3
, this means

\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|\leq L\left(R_{1}+\left\|\mathbf{w}^{\star}\right\|\right)^{s}\text{ for all }{}1\leq t\leq T.\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:gradupperboundestimate}

Once more, by Markov’s Inequality, with probability at least 0.9,

\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{t})\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\leq 10\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{t})\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\right].\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:markovestimate}

Let
E_{2}
be the event that this above inequality holds.

Summing and telescoping from
Lemma
6.5
and using that
\theta\left(\Phi(z)\right)\geq 0
, we obtain

\displaystyle\eta\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{t})\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\theta(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0}))+2C\left(\eta d/\beta\right)^{3/2}T

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\eta^{2}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right]+\frac{2C}{3}\eta^{3}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\|^{3}\right].

Here, we took full expectations over
\{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t},\mathbf{z}_{t}\}_{0\leq t\leq T-1}
in the above.

Using
Lemma
6.4
and taking full expectations, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right]\leq 2\sigma_{F}^{2}+2\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right].

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\|^{3}\right]\leq 8\sigma_{F}^{3}+4\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{3}\right].

Using these in the above we see that

\displaystyle\eta\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{t})\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\theta(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0}))+2C\left(\eta d/\beta\right)^{3/2}T

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\frac{1}{2}\eta^{2}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\left(2\sigma_{F}^{2}+2\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right]\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\frac{2C}{3}\eta^{3}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\left(8\sigma_{F}^{3}+4\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{3}\right]\right).

(31)

Finally, consider
\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{r}\right]
for
r\in\{2,3\}
. The same logic as the proof of
Theorem
6.1
gives that with probability at least 0.975 for a given
r\in\{2,3\}

\displaystyle\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{r}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{r}\right]-2\sqrt{2}\max_{0\leq t\leq T-1}\left(\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{r}\right)\cdot\sqrt{T\log 40}.

(32)

Let
E_{3}
be the intersection of these two events for
r\in\{2,3\}
, so
E_{3}
has probability at least 0.95.

Let

\displaystyle M_{1}(\mathbf{w}_{0})

\displaystyle=10\max\left(\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right),6CB_{\textsc{grad}}^{3}\right),

which is just a
\mathbf{w}_{0}
-dependent constant. (Note compared to the proof of
Theorem
6.1
that the definition of
B_{\textsc{grad}}
changed.)

Now we put these steps together and do a Union Bound over
E_{1},E_{2},E_{3}
. Let
E=E_{1}\cap E_{2}\cap E_{3}
; we have that
E
occurs with probability at least 0.75.

Then conditioned on
E
, combining
(
6.2
)
,
(
6.2
)
,
(
31
)
,
(
32
)
in the same manner we used to prove
Theorem
6.1
, we see

\displaystyle\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{t})\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)

\displaystyle\leq 10\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{t})\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq M_{1}(\mathbf{w}_{0})\left(\frac{1}{\eta}+(d/\beta)^{3/2}\eta^{1/2}T+\eta T+\eta^{2}T+\sqrt{T}\right).

This uses that
B_{\textsc{grad}}=L\left(R_{1}+\left\|\mathbf{w}^{\star}\right\|\right)^{s}+\sigma_{F}
and straightforward estimates.

As before, conditioned on
E
we have

\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\geq\theta^{\prime}\left(\rho_{\Phi}^{-1}\left(\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)\right)\right)\text{ for all }{}0\leq t\leq T-1.

Thus, defining

M(\mathbf{w}_{0})=\frac{M_{1}(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{\theta^{\prime}\left(\rho_{\Phi}^{-1}\left(\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)\right)\right)}\in(0,\infty)

we see that conditioned on
E
which occurs with probability at least 0.75 we have, via identical steps as before,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{t})

\displaystyle\leq 3M(\mathbf{w}_{0})\left(\frac{1}{\eta T}+\eta+d/\beta\right).

We break into cases based on how we set
\eta,T
:

1.

If
\beta\leq\frac{d}{\varepsilon/\left(\log 1/\varepsilon\right)^{5}}
: Now recall we set
\eta=r(\mathbf{w}_{0})\frac{d}{\beta}\leq d/\beta
, and that we let
T
be the floor of the unique solution to the equation

z\log^{7}\left(20z\right)=C(\mathbf{w}_{0})\frac{\beta^{2}}{d^{2}},

where
C(\mathbf{w}_{0})
is defined according to
Lemma
6.6
. Recall we had
T\geq 1
as well as

T\leq C(\mathbf{w}_{0})\frac{\beta^{2}}{d^{2}}\leq\frac{\beta^{2}}{d^{2}}.

Since
T\geq 1
, and as
z\log^{7}(20z)
is increasing for
z\geq 1
, it follows via definition of
T
(note
2\lfloor z\rfloor\geq z
for all
z\geq 1
) that

2T\log^{7}(40T)\geq C(\mathbf{w}_{0})\frac{\beta^{2}}{d^{2}},

hence

\eta T\geq\frac{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{2\log^{7}(40T)}\frac{\beta}{d}.

Thus, we have with probability at least 0.75 that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{t})

\displaystyle\leq 6M(\mathbf{w}_{0})\left(\frac{2^{7}\log^{7}(40)+2^{14}\log^{7}(\beta/d)}{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}+1\right)\frac{d}{\beta}

that is, we obtain
\widetilde{O}(d/\beta)
suboptimality with at most
\frac{\beta^{2}}{d^{2}}
iterations. (This step uses the inequality
(a+b)^{7}\leq 2^{7}(a^{7}+b^{7})
for
a,b\geq 0
.)

2.

If
\beta\geq\frac{d}{\varepsilon/(\log 1/\varepsilon)^{5}}
: Recalling how we set
\eta
and the definition of this case, we have
\eta,d/\beta\leq\varepsilon
. Moreover, note
\eta T\geq\frac{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}{2\varepsilon\left(\log 1/\varepsilon\right)^{3}}
by analogous logic as in the proof of
Theorem
6.1
. Hence, we obtain with probability at least 0.75 that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{t})

\displaystyle\leq 3M(\mathbf{w}_{0})\left(\frac{2\varepsilon(\log 1/\varepsilon)^{3}}{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}+2\varepsilon\right)

\displaystyle\leq 6M(\mathbf{w}_{0})\left(\frac{1}{r(\mathbf{w}_{0})C(\mathbf{w}_{0})}+1\right)\varepsilon\left(\log 1/\varepsilon\right)^{3}.

That is, we obtain
\widetilde{O}(\varepsilon)
suboptimality with at most
T\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\log 1/\varepsilon\right)^{2}
iterations.

∎

6.3
Proof of
Theorem
3.4

We formally state
Theorem
3.4
and the algorithm in question as follows.

Theorem 6.3
.

Suppose the geometric property
(
3
)
holds:

\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}),\nabla F(\mathbf{w})\right\rangle\geq F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w})+\min\left(0,\frac{1}{\beta}\Delta\Phi(\mathbf{w})\right).

Consider running
Algorithm
2
, following the same
\eta,T
as well as cutoff for
\varepsilon
as from
Lemma
6.3
. Then we have the same runtime and error guarantees as
Theorem
6.1
.

Algorithm 2

Modified Langevin Dynamics using Gradient Domination Information

Initialize at
\mathbf{w}_{0}
.

for
each
t\geq 0

do

Compute
\Delta_{t}:=\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}),\nabla F(\mathbf{w})\right\rangle-F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w})
.

if

\Delta_{t}\geq 0

then

\mathbf{w}_{t+1}\leftarrow\mathbf{w}_{t}-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})
.

else
If

\mathbf{w}_{t+1}\leftarrow\mathbf{w}_{t}-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}
where
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\sim\sqrt{d}\mathcal{S}^{d-1}
uniformly. (Recall
\mathcal{S}^{d-1}
is the unit sphere in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
so
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}
is philosophically a Gaussian.)

To prove
Theorem
6.3
, in fact the exact same proof of
Theorem
6.1
will suffice. The main idea is that in all of our bounds involving
\left\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|
, we use either Triangle Inequality or Young’s Inequality to bound
\left\|-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|
and use that
\left\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|\leq\sqrt{d}
. However these results still hold when
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}=0
, when no noise is added. Moreover, this result holds if
F_{\varepsilon}
is replaced with
A
and
A
is query-able, in the same way as described in
Remark
6.1
.

Again we break the proof into similar parts, starting with the one-step discretization bound.

Lemma 6.9
.

For one iteration starting at arbitrary
\mathbf{w}_{t}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}}\left[\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)-\eta\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{t})

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\frac{1}{2}\eta^{2}\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{2}+1_{\mathcal{N}_{t}}\left(\frac{2C}{3}\eta^{3}\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{3}+2C(\eta d/\beta)^{3/2}\right),

where
p
and
C
are defined from
Lemma
6.1
, and where
\mathcal{N}_{t}
is the indicator of if noise was added on round
t
.

Proof.
In rounds where we add noise, we have
\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t}),\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\rangle<F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{t})
. By our condition, this implies we must have

F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\frac{1}{\beta}\Delta\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\leq\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t}),\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\rangle.

Thus, in these rounds this result follows immediately from
Lemma
6.2
.

Otherwise if we do not add noise, we have
\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t}),\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\rangle\geq F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{t})
. The proof now is the same as in
De Sa et al. 2022
. Applying
Lemma
6.1
again, this time for the expansion to at most second order, and using that
\theta^{\prime}\geq 0
, we get from this condition that

\displaystyle\theta\left(\Phi\left(\mathbf{w}_{t+1}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\theta\left(\Phi\left(\mathbf{w}_{t}\right)\right)+\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi\left(\mathbf{w}_{t}\right)\right)\left\langle\nabla\Phi\left(\mathbf{w}_{t}\right),-\eta\nabla F\left(\mathbf{w}_{t}\right)\right\rangle+\frac{\eta^{2}}{2}\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)-\eta\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\frac{\eta^{2}}{2}\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{2},

implying the result.
∎

This result gives us a way to upper bound
\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)
. To control this, we will again need to control the
\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})
which we do as follows.

Lemma 6.10
.

Follow the same notation, assumptions, and choice of
\eta,T
as in
Lemma
6.3
. Then with probability at least
0.9
, we have that

\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\leq\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)\text{ for all }{}0\leq t\leq T-1.

Proof.
Note defining
R\left(\mathbf{w}_{j},\Phi,\eta,\beta,d\right)
identically as in
Lemma
6.3
, we still have from
Lemma
6.9
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}}\left[\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)-\eta\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{t})

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\frac{1}{2}\eta^{2}\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{2}+\frac{2C}{3}\eta^{3}\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{3}+2C(\eta d/\beta)^{3/2}.

Therefore,
Lemma
8.1
still holds.

Moreover, following the same proof we see that
Lemma
8.2
still holds here too, except now
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}
is 0 when
t\in\mathcal{N}_{t}
.

Define
\tau
analogously as in the proof of
Lemma
6.3
. The same derivation as earlier establishes

\displaystyle Y_{\tau}-Y_{0}

\displaystyle\geq\rho_{\Phi}^{-1}\left(\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)\right)-\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})-\left(\frac{3}{2}CB_{\textsc{grad}}^{3}\eta^{2}+2C(\eta d/\beta)^{3/2}\right)T.

Now we use
Lemma
8.2
, which still holds there, to upper bound
Y_{\tau}-Y_{0}
. Denote the event from
Lemma
8.2
with
\delta=0.1
by
E_{1}
. Conditioned on
E_{1}
, which occurs with probability at least
0.9
, we have that

Y_{\tau}-Y_{0}\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}C(\eta,t,d,\beta)^{2}\right)\log(10T)}

where

C(\eta,t,d,\beta)=4\sqrt{\theta\left(\rho_{\Phi}^{-1}\left(\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)\right)\right)}\cdot\left\|-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{\frac{2\eta}{\beta}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|+4\left\|-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{\frac{2\eta}{\beta}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|^{2}.

To control
C(\eta,t,d,\beta,\rho)
, note in the proof of
Lemma
6.3
, we used Triangle Inequality or Young’s Inequality to split up each of the
\left\|-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|
or this quantity squared and isolated the
\left\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|
. Note now that we still have
\left\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|\leq\sqrt{d}
always (
\left\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|=0,\sqrt{d}
), so the same upper bound for
Y_{\tau}-Y_{0}
holds.

Thus the same steps as in the proof of
Lemma
6.3
, with the same choice of
T
and
\eta
, allow us to conclude.
∎

Now, with these parts in hand, we can prove
Theorem
6.3
.

Proof.
The proof is nearly identical to the finish of the proof of
Theorem
6.1
. Again, we can reduce to proving the main case. The only difference is that we now use
Lemma
6.9
, but we can still upper bound the discretization error from that step as
\frac{1}{2}\eta^{2}\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{2}+\frac{2C}{3}\eta^{3}\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{3}+2C(\eta d/\beta)^{3/2}
. Hence we have the same inequality obtained from telescoping as in the proof of
Theorem
6.1
, and the same proof finishes.
∎

6.4
Additional Discussion

We discuss how
Theorem
3.4
implies optimization for PŁ

functions and also KŁ

functions. In particular, they satisfy this condition
(
3
)
: when
\Phi(\mathbf{w})=\lambda F(\mathbf{w})
, we obtain Polyak-Łojasiewicz (PŁ) functions
(
Polyak 1963
;
Lojasiewicz 1963
)
.

Definition 6.1

(Polyak-Łojasiewicz (PŁ))
.

A differentiable function
F
is Polyak-Łojasiewicz (PŁ) if
\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w})\right\|^{2}\geq\lambda F(\mathbf{w})
for some
\lambda>0
, for all
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

PŁ

functions are a classic class of functions for which GD and SGD can be proved to succeed as a strategy for global optimization, but are not necessarily convex. It is well known
\alpha
-strong convexity of
F
implies that
F
satisfies the PŁ

inequality with parameter
2\alpha
, but
not
vice-versa
(
Chewi et al. 2022
)
. Additional examples of PŁ

functions have been found in recent literature, for example transformers
(
Zhang et al. 2024
)
.

Moreover,
(
3
)
can even handle the case of Kurdyka-Łojasiewicz (KŁ) functions
(
Kurdyka 1998
)
, which are a generalization of PŁ

functions.

Definition 6.2

(Kurdyka-Łojasiewicz (KŁ))
.

A differentiable function
F
is Kurdyka-Łojasiewicz (KŁ) if
\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w})\right\|^{2}\geq\lambda F(\mathbf{w})^{1+\theta}
for some
\lambda>0
and
\theta\in[0,1)
for all
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

This can be seen by taking
\Phi(\mathbf{w})=\frac{1}{\lambda(1-\theta)}F(\mathbf{w})^{1-\theta}
(note
(
3
)
can actually handle any
\theta<1
, even if
\theta
is negative). There are many examples of KŁ

functions from generalized linear models
(
Mei et al. 2021
)
to reinforcement learning
(
Agarwal et al. 2021
;
Mei et al. 2020
;
Yuan et al. 2022
)
to over-parametrized nueral networks
(
Zeng et al. 2018
;
Allen-Zhu et al. 2019
)
to low-rank matrix recovery
(
Bi et al. 2022
)
to optimal control
(
Bu et al. 2019
;
Fatkhullin and Polyak 2021
)
.

In all these cases it is reasonable to assume we have query access to
\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}),\nabla F(\mathbf{w})\right\rangle-F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w})
, since
\Phi
solely depends on
F
. Thus we obtain the following corollary:

Corollary 1
.

Suppose
F
is PŁ

/KŁ

and satisfies
Assumption
2.2
,
Assumption
3.1
, and
Assumption
3.2
(the latter two assumptions with
F
in place of
\Phi
). Then running
Algorithm
2
with constant step size, we can optimize PŁ

and KŁ

functions to any precision
\varepsilon\geq\widetilde{\Omega}\left(\frac{d}{\beta}\right)
in
\widetilde{O}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}})
iterations.

However, note
(
3
)
is much looser than PŁ

and KŁ

functions. PŁ

and KŁ

functions here correspond to when we
never
add noise in
Algorithm
2
. Thus we believe
(
3
)
encompasses many more non-convex optimization problems of interest.

7
Proofs for
Section
2

In this section, as with
Section
6
, we state all guarantees with constant probability. To obtain those results with probability
1-\delta
, one can simply use the standard log-boosting trick.

7.1
Proofs of
Theorem
2.2
,
2.3
, and
2.4

Here we formally state and prove
Theorem
2.2
,
2.3
, and
2.4
, which are all subsumed by the following result.

Theorem 7.1
.

Suppose that
F
satisfies
Assumption
2.2
and
Assumption
2.3
.
Suppose
\mu_{\beta}
has second moment
S<\infty
and satisfies a Poincaré Inequality with constant
\textsf{C}_{\textsc{pi}}(\mu_{\beta})
with
\beta=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)
, namely

\varepsilon\geq\frac{2d}{\beta}\log(4\pi e\beta LdS).

Suppose
\Phi
(from
Theorem
2.1
) satisfies
Assumption
2.1
with
0\leq p\leq 1
, and define
\rho_{\Phi}=\max\left(\rho_{\Phi,1},\rho_{\Phi,2},\rho_{\Phi,3}\right)
. We can assume without loss of generality that
\rho_{\Phi}(z)=A(z+1)^{p}
for some constant
A>0
.

Then consider running either
GLD
, or
SGLD
using a stochastic gradient oracle
\nabla f
satisfying
Assumption
2.4
and
Assumption
2.5
, with constant step size for
T
iterations.
We will reach a
\mathbf{w}
in
\{\mathbf{w}:F(\mathbf{w})\leq\varepsilon\}
with probability at least 0.8 in at most
T
gradient (for
GLD
) or stochastic gradient (for
SGLD
) evaluations respectively, where we set

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\leq 8^{3}C_{0}\max\left\{1,\frac{4L^{2}}{m},\frac{4\max(L,B)}{m},\frac{4B^{2}}{m},6B,120^{2}A^{2}B^{2}M^{2},120AC_{0}M\right\}

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt\cdot\max\left\{\beta\max\left(\textsf{C}_{\textsc{pi}}(\mu_{\beta}),2\right),d^{3}\max\left(\textsf{C}_{\textsc{pi}}(\mu_{\beta}),2\right)^{3},\beta^{2+s/2}\max\left(\textsf{C}_{\textsc{pi}}(\mu_{\beta}),2\right)^{2+s/2}\right\}.

(An explicit expression can be found in our proof.)

Here we define the above constants as follows:

L_{2}:=\left(\left\|\mathbf{w}_{0}\right\|^{4}+8\left(\frac{4\left(m+b+\frac{4d+2}{\beta}\right)}{m\wedge 1}\right)^{\frac{1+\gamma}{\gamma}\lor 2}\right)^{s/2},L_{3}:=\left(\left\|\mathbf{w}_{0}\right\|^{4}+8\left(\frac{4\left(m+b+\frac{4d+2}{\beta}\right)}{m\wedge 1}\right)^{\frac{1+\gamma}{\gamma}\lor 2}\right)^{3s/4},

B=\max\left(L\max(1,\|\mathbf{w}^{\star}\|),\sigma_{F}\right),C_{0}=50A\theta(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0}))\lor 1,C=\frac{4A^{2}p(p+1)+2Ap+1}{3},

M=\max\left(\frac{1}{2},2C\right)\cdot\left(8\sigma_{F}^{3}+16\max(L,B)^{3}(\max\left(L_{2},L_{3}\right)+1)\right).

Here
\theta=\frac{1}{\rho_{\Phi}}
, as defined in
Lemma
6.1
. (Take
L\leftarrow\max(1,L)
,
\sigma_{F}\leftarrow\max(\sigma_{F},1)
if necessary.)

Moreover, this generalizes to
s=0,1
as follows:

1.

In the case when
s=0
, this result holds with no dependence on
L_{2},L_{3}
and instead we have

M=\max\left(\frac{1}{2},2C\right)\cdot\left(8\sigma_{F}^{3}+16\max(L,B)^{3}\right).

2.

In the case when
s=1
, we no longer need to make assumptions on
\mu_{\beta}
: as
2s\leq\gamma\leq s+1
,
s=1
forces
\gamma=1
, the setting of
F
being
L
-smooth and
(m,b)
dissipative from
Raginsky et al. 2017
,
Xu et al. 2018
, and
Zou et al. 2021
. As shown in
Raginsky et al. 2017
, these conditions imply
\mu_{\beta}
satisfies a Poincaré Inequality for
\beta\geq\frac{2}{m}
, and also that
\mu_{\beta}
has finite second moment
S\leq\frac{b+d/\beta}{m}
.

Moreover, our guarantees improve as follows. Instead letting

L_{2}:=\left\|\mathbf{w}_{0}\right\|^{2}+\frac{2}{m}\left(b+2B^{2}+\frac{d}{\beta}\right),L_{3}:=\left(\left\|\mathbf{w}_{0}\right\|^{4}+C^{\prime\prime}\lor\frac{2C^{\prime\prime}}{m}\right)^{3/4},

where

C^{\prime\prime}=\frac{4}{m}\left(2C^{\prime 2}\left(4+\frac{1}{m}\right)\lor\frac{1}{m}\left(3mB+C^{\prime}\right)^{2}\right),C^{\prime}=m+b+\frac{4d+2}{\beta},

we have a runtime guarantee of

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\leq 8^{3}C_{0}\max\left\{1,\frac{4L^{2}}{m},\frac{4\max(L,B)}{m},\frac{4B^{2}}{m},6B,120^{2}A^{2}B^{2}M^{2},120AC_{0}M\right\}

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt\cdot\max\left\{\beta\max\left(\textsf{C}_{\textsc{pi}}(\mu_{\beta}),2\right),d^{3}\max\left(\textsf{C}_{\textsc{pi}}(\mu_{\beta}),2\right)^{3},\beta^{2}\max\left(\textsf{C}_{\textsc{pi}}(\mu_{\beta}),2\right)^{2}\right\}.

First, note from our assumption that
\varepsilon\geq\frac{2d}{\beta}\log(4\pi e\beta LdS)
, we may apply
Theorem
2.1
(in particular, by combining
Theorem
5.1
,
Lemma
5.1
) to obtain
\Phi
satisfying the properties described in
Theorem
2.1
.

Next, we show a Lemma showing the iterates of GLD and SGLD are controlled. We will need this only when
s>0
. Similar results have been shown in
Raginsky et al. 2017
and
Balasubramanian et al. 2022
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Suppose
F
satisfies
Assumption
2.2
and
Assumption
2.3
. Consider the
\{\mathbf{w}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
generated by
GLD
/
SGLD
(for
SGLD
we need
Assumption
2.5
), run for
T
iterations for
T<\infty
(we only use this for the
T
we set later). If the step size
\eta\in(0,1\wedge\frac{m}{4L^{2}}\wedge\frac{m}{4\max(L,B)}\wedge\frac{m}{4B^{2}}\wedge\frac{1}{6B})
, then we have the following bounds:

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{2s}\right]\leq L_{2}\max(\eta T,1)^{s/2},\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{3s}\right]\leq L_{3}\max(\eta T,1)^{3s/4},

where we define

L_{2}:=\left(\left\|\mathbf{w}_{0}\right\|^{4}+8\left(\frac{4\left(m+b+\frac{4d+2}{\beta}\right)}{m\wedge 1}\right)^{\frac{1+\gamma}{\gamma}\lor 2}\right)^{s/2},L_{3}:=\left(\left\|\mathbf{w}_{0}\right\|^{4}+8\left(\frac{4\left(m+b+\frac{4d+2}{\beta}\right)}{m\wedge 1}\right)^{\frac{1+\gamma}{\gamma}\lor 2}\right)^{3s/4}.

Here
B=\max\left(L\max(1,\|\mathbf{w}^{\star}\|),\sigma_{F}\right)
, where
\sigma_{F}
comes from
Assumption
2.4
. (Recall we took
L\leftarrow\max\left(L,1\right)
if necessary earlier in the statement of
Theorem
7.1
.)

Moreover, if
s=1
(which implies
F
is
L
-smooth and
(m,b)
dissipative), we have the following uniform bounds:

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right]\leq L_{2},\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{3}\right]\leq L_{3}

for

L_{2}:=\left\|\mathbf{w}_{0}\right\|^{2}+\frac{2}{m}\left(b+2B^{2}+\frac{d}{\beta}\right),L_{3}:=\left(\left\|\mathbf{w}_{0}\right\|^{4}+C^{\prime\prime}\lor\frac{2C^{\prime\prime}}{m}\right)^{3/4}

where

C^{\prime\prime}=\frac{4}{m}\left(2C^{\prime 2}\left(4+\frac{1}{m}\right)\lor\frac{1}{m}\left(3mB+C^{\prime}\right)^{2}\right),C^{\prime}=m+b+\frac{4d+2}{\beta}.

Proof.
Our goal is to use Proposition 14 of
Balasubramanian et al. 2022
to control the second and fourth moments of the
\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|
. Intuitively, our result should be the same as theirs except their
V
is replaced with
\beta F
, and then the relevant parameters change (except for
d
, the rest of them are all scaled by
\beta
). However, this gives some unnecessary
\beta
dependence which arises for technical reasons in their analysis (intuitively, they should cancel), so we need to modify their proof slightly to improve this dependence.

As done in the sampling literature
(
Chewi 2024
)
, define the continuous-time interpolation of
(
1
)
by

\mathbf{w}_{r}=\mathbf{w}_{t}-(r-t\eta)\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\left(\mathbf{B}(r)-\mathbf{B}(t\eta)\right)\text{ for all }{}r\in[t\eta,(t+1)\eta).

This appears somewhat different than the interpolation defined in the literature, but it is actually the same. Our process
(
1
)
with step size
\eta
is equivalent to theirs with their
V=\beta F
and their step size
h=\frac{\eta}{\beta}
. They index by ‘time’ where the subscript
th
corresponds to the
t
-th iterate whereas we index iterates simply by the iteration count (which is at ‘time’
t\eta
in the above interpolation) and are indexing time by
r
to avoid confusion.
8
8

8

Using this correspondence one can actually carefully track the proof of Proposition 14 of
Balasubramanian et al. 2022
to show a similar result to what we show here.

For the stochastic gradient case, this will be instead

\mathbf{w}_{r}=\mathbf{w}_{t}-(r-t\eta)\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})+\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\left(\mathbf{B}(r)-\mathbf{B}(t\eta)\right)\text{ for all }{}r\in[t\eta,(t+1)\eta).

Note for both these interpolations, all functions of quantities at time
t\eta
/iteration count
t
are constant (including
\mathbf{z}_{t}
), the randomness being over the Brownian motion
\mathbf{B}(r)-\mathbf{B}(t\eta)
.

We will do the proofs in the stochastic gradient case, and the proofs in the exact gradient case are the exact same.

First, we control the second moment. Let
\mathfrak{F}_{t}
be defined identically as in
Section
6
. Analogously to the proof of Proposition 14 of
Balasubramanian et al. 2022
, Itô’s Lemma applied to
\left\|\mathbf{w}\right\|^{2}
conditioned on
\mathfrak{F}_{t}
yields for all
r\in[t\eta,(t+1)\eta]
,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{r}\right\|^{2}|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]

\displaystyle=2\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\mathbf{w}_{r},-\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\rangle|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}^{2}\cdot 2\text{tr}(\mathbb{I}_{d})

\displaystyle=-2\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\mathbf{w}_{t}-(r-t\eta)\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})+\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\left(\mathbf{B}(r)-\mathbf{B}(t\eta)\right),\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\rangle|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]+\frac{2d}{\beta}

\displaystyle=-2\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\mathbf{w}_{t}-(r-t\eta)\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t}),\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t}\right\rangle|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]+\frac{2d}{\beta}

\displaystyle\leq 2b-2m\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{\gamma}+2(r-t\eta)\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\|^{2}+\frac{2d}{\beta}

\displaystyle\leq 2b-2m\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{\gamma}+4\eta\cdot L^{2}\max(1,\left\|\mathbf{w}^{\star}\right\|)^{2s}\left(\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{2s}+1\right)+\frac{2d}{\beta}

\displaystyle\leq 4m+2b+\frac{2d}{\beta}.

(33)

In the above we use that
\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\left(\mathbf{B}(r)-\mathbf{B}(t\eta)\right),\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\rangle|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]=0
,
Assumption
2.5
,
Lemma
8.3
,
\gamma\geq 2s
,
\eta\leq\frac{m}{2B^{2}}
, and
r-t\eta\leq\eta
. Integrating this over
r\in[t\eta,(t+1)\eta]
and iterating yields

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right]\leq\left\|\mathbf{w}_{0}\right\|^{2}+\left(4m+2b+\frac{2d}{\beta}\right)\cdot\eta t\leq\left(\left\|\mathbf{w}_{0}\right\|^{2}+4m+2b+\frac{2d}{\beta}\right)\max(\eta T,1).

We now control the fourth moment with the same idea. Applying Itô’s Lemma to
\left\|\mathbf{w}\right\|^{4}=\left(\left\|\mathbf{w}\right\|^{2}\right)^{2}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{r}\right\|^{4}|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]

\displaystyle=-4\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{r}\right\|^{2}\left\langle\mathbf{w}_{r},\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\rangle|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}^{2}\cdot(4d+2)\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{r}\right\|^{2}|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]

\displaystyle=-4\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{r}\right\|^{2}\left\langle\mathbf{w}_{t}-(r-t\eta)\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})+\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\left(\mathbf{B}(r)-\mathbf{B}(t\eta)\right),\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\rangle|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]+\frac{4d+2}{\beta}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{r}\right\|^{2}|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right].

Let
\mathbf{x}=\frac{\mathbf{B}(r)-\mathbf{B}(t\eta)}{\sqrt{r-t\eta}}
be a standard Gaussian vector. Using Gaussian Integration by Parts on
h(\mathbf{x})=\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}-(r-t\eta)\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})+\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\cdot\sqrt{r-t\eta}\mathbf{x}\right\|^{2}=\left\|\mathbf{w}_{r}\right\|^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{r}\right\|^{2}\left\langle\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\left(\mathbf{B}(r)-\mathbf{B}(t\eta)\right),\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\rangle|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\cdot\sqrt{r-t\eta}\cdot\left\langle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{x}h(\mathbf{x})|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right],\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\cdot\sqrt{r-t\eta}\cdot\left\langle\mathbb{E}\left[\nabla h(\mathbf{x})|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right],\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{\beta}(r-t\eta)\left\langle\mathbf{w}_{t}-(r-t\eta)\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t}),\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\rangle.

The above follows since
\nabla h(\mathbf{x})=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\cdot\sqrt{r-t\eta}\cdot\left(\mathbf{w}_{t}-(r-t\eta)\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})+\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\cdot\sqrt{r-t\eta}\mathbf{x}\right)
and as
\mathbf{x}
is independent of
\mathfrak{F}_{t}
and has mean of the 0 vector.

Hence, we have for all
r\in[t\eta,(t+1)\eta]
,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{r}\right\|^{4}|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]

\displaystyle=4\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{r}\right\|^{2}|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]\left(-\left\langle\mathbf{w}_{t},\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\rangle+(r-t\eta)\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\|^{2}+\frac{4d+2}{\beta}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt-\frac{16}{\beta}(r-t\eta)\left\langle\mathbf{w}_{t}-(r-t\eta)\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t}),\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leq 4\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{r}\right\|^{2}|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]+\frac{4}{\beta}(r-t\eta)\right)\left(-\left\langle\mathbf{w}_{t},\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\rangle+(r-t\eta)\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\|^{2}+\frac{4d+2}{\beta}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 4\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{r}\right\|^{2}|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]+\frac{4}{\beta}(r-t\eta)\right)\left(-m\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{\gamma}+b+2\eta\cdot L^{2}\max(1,\left\|\mathbf{w}^{\star}\right\|)^{2s}\left(\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{2s}+1\right)+\frac{4d+2}{\beta}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 4\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{r}\right\|^{2}|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]+\frac{4}{\beta}(r-t\eta)\right)\left(-\frac{m}{2}\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{\gamma}+m+b+\frac{4d+2}{\beta}\right).

(34)

The above follows as
r\geq t\eta
and so the first factor in the above is always non-negative, as well as
\eta\leq\frac{m}{4B^{2}}
and
\gamma\geq 2s
.

Define
C^{\prime}:=m+b+\frac{4d+2}{\beta}
for convenience. If
\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|\geq\left(\frac{2C^{\prime}}{m}\right)^{1/\gamma}
, this means
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{r}\right\|^{4}|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]\leq 0
. Otherwise if
\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|\leq\left(\frac{2C^{\prime}}{m}\right)^{1/\gamma}
, using our upper bound on
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{r}\right\|^{2}|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]
gives

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{r}\right\|^{2}|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]+\frac{4}{\beta}(r-t\eta)\leq\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{2}+\left(4m+2b+\frac{2d}{\beta}\right)(r-t\eta)+\frac{4}{\beta}(r-t\eta)\leq\left(\frac{2C^{\prime}}{m}\right)^{1/\gamma}+4C^{\prime},

as
r-t\eta\leq\eta\leq 1
. Note
\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|\leq\left(\frac{2C^{\prime}}{m}\right)^{1/\gamma}
implies the second factor in
(
34
)
is non-negative, and the second factor is at most
C^{\prime}
clearly. Thus, in this case we have

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{r}\right\|^{4}|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]\leq 4C^{\prime}\left(\left(\frac{2C^{\prime}}{m}\right)^{1/\gamma}+4C^{\prime}\right)\leq 8\left(\frac{4C^{\prime}}{m\wedge 1}\right)^{\frac{1+\gamma}{\gamma}\lor 2}.

Hence the above is an upper bound on
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{r}\right\|^{4}|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]
in all cases, and iterating this gives the desired fourth moment bound

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{4}\right]\leq\left\|\mathbf{w}_{0}\right\|^{4}+8\left(\frac{4C^{\prime}}{m\wedge 1}\right)^{\frac{1+\gamma}{\gamma}\lor 2}\eta t\leq\left(\left\|\mathbf{w}_{0}\right\|^{4}+8\left(\frac{4C^{\prime}}{m\wedge 1}\right)^{\frac{1+\gamma}{\gamma}\lor 2}\right)\max(\eta T,1).

From here, to obtain the desired conclusion, use monotonicity of moments (as
s\leq 1
):

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{2s}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{4}\right]^{2s/4}\leq\left(\left\|\mathbf{w}_{0}\right\|^{4}+8\left(\frac{4C^{\prime}}{m\wedge 1}\right)^{\frac{1+\gamma}{\gamma}\lor 2}\right)^{s/2}\max(\eta T,1)^{s/2}.

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{3s}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{4}\right]^{3s/4}\leq\left(\left\|\mathbf{w}_{0}\right\|^{4}+8\left(\frac{4C^{\prime}}{m\wedge 1}\right)^{\frac{1+\gamma}{\gamma}\lor 2}\right)^{3s/4}\max(\eta T,1)^{3s/4}.

When
s=1
and hence
\gamma=2
(which implies
(m,b)
dissipativeness), we can be tighter in the above analysis. First, using
Lemma
6.4
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{z}_{t}}\left[\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})-\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right]\leq\sigma_{F}^{2}\leq B^{2}.

With the above, identically as the steps of the proof of Lemma 3 of
Raginsky et al. 2017
, using
(m,b)
dissipativeness and our constant upper bound on
\eta
, we can show a uniform bound on the second moment for both exact and stochastic gradients:

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right]\leq\left\|\mathbf{w}_{0}\right\|^{2}+\frac{2}{m}\left(b+2B^{2}+\frac{d}{\beta}\right).

We also claim we have a uniform upper bound on the fourth moment. We break into two cases, both using a similar strategy.

1.

\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|\leq\left(\frac{2C^{\prime}}{m}\right)^{1/2}
: In this case, the second factor in
(
34
)
is non-negative. Recall the upper bound we showed from
(
33
)
:

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{r}\right\|^{2}|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]\leq 4m+2b+\frac{2d}{\beta}.

This implies

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{r}\right\|^{2}|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]\leq\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{2}+\left(4m+2b+\frac{2d}{\beta}\right)(r-t\eta).

Now as the second factor in
(
34
)
is non-negative, we obtain using
r-t\eta\leq\eta\leq 1
,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{r}\right\|^{4}|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 4\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{r}\right\|^{2}|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]+\frac{4}{\beta}(r-t\eta)\right)\left(-\frac{m}{2}\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{2}+m+b+\frac{4d+2}{\beta}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 4\left(\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{2}+\left(4m+2b+\frac{2d}{\beta}\right)(r-t\eta)+\frac{4}{\beta}(r-t\eta)\right)\left(-\frac{m}{2}\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{2}+m+b+\frac{4d+2}{\beta}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 4\left(\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{2}+4C^{\prime}\right)\left(-\frac{m}{2}\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{2}+C^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 4\left(-\frac{m}{2}\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{4}+C^{\prime}\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{2}+4C^{\prime 2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 4\left(-\frac{m}{4}\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{4}+C^{\prime 2}\left(4+\frac{1}{m}\right)\right)=-m\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{4}+4C^{\prime 2}\left(4+\frac{1}{m}\right).

The last step uses AM-GM.

2.

\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|>\left(\frac{2C^{\prime}}{m}\right)^{1/2}
: This time, the second factor in
(
34
)
is negative, so we aim to lower bound
\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{r}\right\|^{2}|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]
. Recalling the intermediate steps in
(
33
)
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{r}\right\|^{2}|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]

\displaystyle=-2\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\mathbf{w}_{t}-(r-t\eta)\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t}),\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t}\right\rangle|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]+\frac{2d}{\beta}

\displaystyle\geq-2\left\langle\mathbf{w}_{t},\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\rangle

\displaystyle\geq-2\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\|

\displaystyle\geq-2B\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|\left(\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|+1\right)

\displaystyle\geq-3B\left(\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{2}+1\right),

where we upper bound
\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})
via
Lemma
8.3
and use AM-GM in the last step.

This implies

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{r}\right\|^{2}|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]\geq\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{2}-3B\left(\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{2}+1\right)(r-t\eta)\geq\frac{1}{2}\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{2}-3B,

since
r-t\eta\leq\eta
,
\eta\leq\frac{1}{6B}\leq 1
.

The second factor in
(
34
)
is negative, so we may apply this in
(
34
)
to give

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{r}\right\|^{4}|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 4\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{r}\right\|^{2}|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]+\frac{4}{\beta}(r-t\eta)\right)\left(-\frac{m}{2}\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{2}+m+b+\frac{4d+2}{\beta}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 4\left(\frac{1}{2}\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{2}-3B\right)\left(-\frac{m}{2}\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{2}+C^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=4\left(-\frac{m}{4}\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{4}+\left(\frac{3mB}{2}+\frac{C^{\prime}}{2}\right)\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{2}-3BC^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 4\left(-\frac{m}{8}\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{4}+\frac{(3mB+C^{\prime})^{2}}{2m}\right)=-\frac{m}{2}\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{4}+\frac{2}{m}\cdot(3mB+C^{\prime})^{2}.

Again, the last step uses AM-GM.

From the above we see that in either case we have

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{r}\right\|^{4}|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]\leq-\frac{m}{2}\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{4}+C^{\prime\prime}

where
C^{\prime\prime}=4C^{\prime 2}\left(4+\frac{1}{m}\right)\lor\frac{2}{m}\cdot(3mB+C^{\prime})^{2}
.

Iterating the above for one step and then taking full expectation yields the recursion

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t+1}\right\|^{4}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{4}\right]+\eta\left(-\frac{m}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{4}\right]+C^{\prime\prime}\right)=\left(1-\frac{\eta m}{2}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{4}\right]+\eta C^{\prime\prime}.

If
1-\frac{\eta m}{2}\leq 0
we obtain
\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{4}\right]\leq C^{\prime\prime}
, and otherwise if
1-\frac{\eta m}{2}\in(0,1)
, iterating the above and summing the resulting geometric series gives

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{4}\right]\leq\left(1-\frac{\eta m}{2}\right)^{t}\left\|\mathbf{w}_{0}\right\|^{4}+\frac{2\eta C^{\prime\prime}}{\eta m}\leq\left\|\mathbf{w}_{0}\right\|^{4}+\frac{2C^{\prime\prime}}{m}.

The desired upper bound on the third moment in this case now just comes from monotonicity of moments.
∎

We now are ready to prove
Theorem
7.1
. We do the proof when
0<s\leq 1
(when
s>0
,
\gamma\geq 2s>0
so we can certainly use
Lemma
7.1
), and we discuss the simple extension to
s=0
and the tighter results when
s=1
at the end.

Proof.
Consider
\theta
and
C=\frac{Ap^{2}+4Ap+1}{6}
defined in terms of
\rho_{\Phi}
in

for the

case.

We set

C_{0}=50A\theta(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0}))\lor 1,M=\max\left(\frac{1}{2},2C\right)\cdot\left(8\sigma_{F}^{3}+16\max(L,B)^{3}(\max\left(L_{2},L_{3}\right)+1)\right),

\eta=\min\left(1,\frac{m}{4L^{2}},\frac{m}{4\max(L,B)},\frac{m}{4B^{2}},\frac{1}{6B},\frac{1}{120^{2}A^{2}B^{2}M^{2}}\cdot\frac{\beta^{3}\lambda^{2}}{d^{3}},\frac{\lambda^{1+s/2}}{120AC_{0}M}\right),

T=\frac{C_{0}}{\eta\lambda}.

Here
\lambda\in\left[\frac{1}{8\beta}\min\left(\frac{1}{{\textsf{C}_{\textsc{pi}}}(\mu_{\beta})},\frac{1}{2}\right),\frac{1}{4\beta}\min\left(\frac{1}{{\textsf{C}_{\textsc{pi}}}(\mu_{\beta})},\frac{1}{2}\right)\right]
, as with
\Phi
, comes from

. Thus, using

T=C_{0}\max\left\{\frac{1}{\lambda}\max\left(1,\frac{4L^{2}}{m},\frac{4\max(L,B)}{m},\frac{4B^{2}}{m},6B\right),120^{2}A^{2}B^{2}M^{2}\cdot\frac{d^{3}}{\beta^{3}\lambda^{3}},120AC_{0}M\cdot\frac{1}{\lambda^{2+s/2}}\right\}

and

\frac{1}{\lambda}\leq 8\beta\max\left(\textsf{C}_{\textsc{pi}}(\mu_{\beta}),2\right),

we see that our definition of
T
above is consistent with the statement of
Theorem
7.1
. Moreover, note
\eta T=\frac{C_{0}}{\lambda}\geq 1
.

As with before let
\mathfrak{F}_{t}
be the natural filtration with respect to
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t^{\prime}},\mathbf{z}_{t^{\prime}}
for all

in the SGLD case, and with respect to
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t^{\prime}}
for all

in the GLD case.

Define

\tau_{\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon},T}(\mathbf{w}_{0})=\min\left(\tau_{\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}}(\mathbf{w}_{0}),T\right),

where in a slight abuse of notation,

now denotes the hitting time of discrete-time

/

to
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
with the choice of
\eta
above. Note

.

Consider
\mathbf{w}_{t}
for

, thus

. By

, this implies for this
\mathbf{w}_{t}
,
(
2.1
)
holds:

\left\langle\nabla F(\mathbf{w}),\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w})\right\rangle\geq\lambda\Phi(\mathbf{w})+\frac{1}{\beta}\Delta\Phi(\mathbf{w}).

Recall

from

, including in this case where

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t},\mathbf{z}_{t}}\left[\theta(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1}))\right]

\displaystyle\leq\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)-\eta\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\cdot\lambda\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\frac{1}{2}\eta^{2}\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{2}+C\eta^{3}\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{3}+2C\left(\frac{\eta d}{\beta}\right)^{3/2}.

This uses

In the stochastic gradient case we analogously have via the same logic as

that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t},\mathbf{z}_{t}}\left[\theta(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1}))\right]

\displaystyle\leq\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)-\eta\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\cdot\lambda\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\frac{1}{2}\eta^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{z}_{t}}\left[\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right]+C\eta^{3}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{z}_{t}}\left[\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w}_{t};\mathbf{z}_{t})\right\|^{3}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+2C\left(\frac{\eta d}{\beta}\right)^{3/2}.

(Note these results can be proved for either

or

by the exact same proof as

,
Lemma

.)

Applying

and then

, Young’s Inequality, and
\left\|\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{b}\right\|^{3}\leq 4\left\|\mathbf{a}\right\|^{3}+4\left\|\mathbf{b}\right\|^{3}
, and noting

, we see in both the

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t},\mathbf{z}_{t}}\left[\theta(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1}))\right]

\displaystyle\leq\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)-\eta\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\cdot\lambda\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\frac{1}{2}\eta^{2}\left(2\sigma_{F}^{2}+2\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right)+C\eta^{3}\left(8\sigma_{F}^{3}+4\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{3}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+2C\left(\frac{\eta d}{\beta}\right)^{3/2}

\displaystyle\leq\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)-\eta\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\cdot\lambda\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\frac{1}{2}\eta^{2}\left(2\sigma_{F}^{2}+4\max(L,B)^{2}\left(\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{2s}+1\right)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+C\eta^{3}\left(8\sigma_{F}^{3}+16\max(L,B)^{3}\left(\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{3s}+1\right)\right)+2C\left(\frac{\eta d}{\beta}\right)^{3/2}.

Recall that
\theta^{\prime}(z)=\frac{1}{A(z+1)^{p}}
where

, which is increasing on

. Therefore,
z\theta^{\prime}(z)=\frac{z}{A(z+1)^{p}}\geq\frac{1}{2A}
for

. Recall
\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\geq 1
from

, because
