. Thus,
\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\geq\frac{1}{2A}
. Therefore we can rearrange the above as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t},\mathbf{z}_{t}}\left[\theta(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1}))\right]

\displaystyle\leq\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)-\eta\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\cdot\lambda\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\frac{1}{2}\eta^{2}\left(2\sigma_{F}^{2}+4\max(L,B)^{2}\left(\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{2s}+1\right)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+C\eta^{3}\left(8\sigma_{F}^{3}+16\max(L,B)^{3}\left(\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{3s}+1\right)\right)+2C\left(\frac{\eta d}{\beta}\right)^{3/2}

\displaystyle\leq\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)-\frac{\eta\lambda}{2A}+\frac{1}{2}\eta^{2}\left(2\sigma_{F}^{2}+4\max(L,B)^{2}\left(\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{2s}+1\right)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+C\eta^{3}\left(8\sigma_{F}^{3}+16\max(L,B)^{3}\left(\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{3s}+1\right)\right)+2C\left(\frac{\eta d}{\beta}\right)^{3/2}

\displaystyle=\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)-\frac{\eta\lambda}{2A}+\text{err}(\mathbf{w}_{t}),

(35)

where we define

\text{err}(\mathbf{w}):=\frac{1}{2}\eta^{2}\left(2\sigma_{F}^{2}+4\max(L,B)^{2}\left(\left\|\mathbf{w}\right\|^{2s}+1\right)\right)+C\eta^{3}\left(8\sigma_{F}^{3}+16\max(L,B)^{3}\left(\left\|\mathbf{w}\right\|^{3s}+1\right)\right)+2C\left(\frac{\eta d}{\beta}\right)^{3/2}>0.

Now with
(
35
)
, the idea is to sum and telescope this relations over
\tau_{\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon},T+1}
time steps, as discussed in
Subsection
2.1
. The way to do this is using discrete-time Dynkin’s Formula, stated in Theorem 11.3.1 of
Meyn and Tweedie 2012
:

Theorem 7.2

(Theorem 11.3.1 of
Meyn and Tweedie 2012
)
.

Let
Z_{t}
be any
\mathfrak{F}_{t}
-measurable function of
\mathbf{w}_{0},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{t}
. Consider any stopping time
\tau
and define
\tau^{n}:=\min\left\{n,\tau,\inf\left(t\geq 0:\mathbf{z}_{t}\geq n\right)\right\}
. Then we have for all
n\geq 0
and
\mathbf{w}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
that

\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{\tau^{n}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{0}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{\tau^{n}}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{t}|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]-Z_{t-1}\right)\right].

As a simple corollary of
Theorem
7.2
, we have the following, Proposition 11.3.2 of
Meyn and Tweedie 2012
. Unlike the above, it holds for
any stopping time
.

Corollary 2

(Proposition 11.3.2 of
Meyn and Tweedie 2012
)
.

Suppose there exists non-negative functions
s_{t},f_{t}
9
9

9

The result in
Meyn and Tweedie 2012
states this for positive
s_{t},f_{t}
, but it is clear their proof still works when the functions are non-negative.
such that

\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{t+1}|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]\leq Z_{t}-f_{t}\left(\mathbf{w}_{t}\right)+s_{t}\left(\mathbf{w}_{t}\right).\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:onesteprecursionfordynkin}

Then for any
\mathbf{w}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any stopping time
\tau
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}f_{t}\left(\mathbf{w}_{t}\right)\right]\leq Z_{0}+\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\tau-1}s_{t}\left(\mathbf{w}_{t}\right)\right].

Apply
Corollary
2
for the stopping time
\tau=\tau_{\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon},T+1}
,
Z_{t}=\theta\left(\Phi\left(\mathbf{w}_{t}\right)\right)
, and the functions
f_{t},s_{t}
defined as follows. Take

f_{t}(\mathbf{w})=\begin{cases}\frac{\eta\lambda}{2A}&\text{ if }{}\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}^{c}\\
0&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}.

In the
GLD
case take

s_{t}(\mathbf{w})=\begin{cases}\text{err}(\mathbf{w})&\text{ if }{}\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}^{c}\\
\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\mathbf{z}}\left[\theta\left(\Phi\left(\mathbf{w}-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w})+\sqrt{\frac{2\eta}{\beta}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\right)\right)\right]&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases},

and in the
SGLD
case take

s_{t}(\mathbf{w})=\begin{cases}\text{err}(\mathbf{w})&\text{ if }{}\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}^{c}\\
\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},\mathbf{z}}\left[\theta\left(\Phi\left(\mathbf{w}-\eta\nabla f(\mathbf{w};\mathbf{z})+\sqrt{\frac{2\eta}{\beta}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\right)\right)\right]&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}.

where
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbb{I}_{d})
and
\mathbf{z}
is an arbitrary data sample. Note the
\{f_{t}\}
, as well as the
\{s_{t}\}
, are the same function for all
t
. Since
\theta\geq 0
, the
f_{t}
and
s_{t}
are non-negative. As
\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t},\mathbf{z}_{t}}\left[\cdot\right]
is the same as
\mathbb{E}\left[\cdot|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]
,
(
35
)
proves that
(
2
)
holds if
\mathbf{w}_{t}\in\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}^{c}
, and
(
2
)
holds for
\mathbf{w}_{t}\in\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
as the
Z_{t}\geq 0
and as the
s_{t}(\mathbf{w}_{t})=\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{t+1}|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]
10
10

10

But this is not relevant, since we apply
Corollary
2
with
\tau=\tau_{\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon},T+1}
.
. Thus,
Corollary
2
yields

\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\tau_{\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon},T}\left(\mathbf{w}_{0}\right)-1}\frac{\eta\lambda}{2A}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\tau_{\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon},T}\left(\mathbf{w}_{0}\right)-1}f_{t}\left(\mathbf{w}_{t}\right)\right]\leq Z_{0}+\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\tau_{\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon},T}\left(\mathbf{w}_{0}\right)-1}s_{t}\left(\mathbf{w}_{t}\right)\right]=\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)+\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\tau_{\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon},T}\left(\mathbf{w}_{0}\right)-1}\text{err}(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right],

since
\mathbf{w}_{t}\in\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}^{c}
for all
t\leq\tau_{\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon},T}\left(\mathbf{w}_{0}\right)-1
, and using the definition of
f_{t},s_{t}
in that case.

Clearly we can simplify the left hand side as
\frac{\eta\lambda}{2A}\mathbb{E}\left[\tau_{\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon},T}\left(\mathbf{w}_{0}\right)\right]
. For the right hand side, note pointwise we have
\sum_{t=0}^{\tau_{\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon},T}\left(\mathbf{w}_{0}\right)-1}\text{err}(\mathbf{w}_{t})\leq\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\text{err}(\mathbf{w}_{t})
by definition of
\tau_{\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon},T}\left(\mathbf{w}_{0}\right)
and as the
\text{err}(\mathbf{w})\geq 0
. Moreover, all the relevant expectations are finite (by
Lemma
7.1
and as
\tau_{\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon},T+1}\leq T<\infty
). Therefore we see

\frac{\eta\lambda}{2A}\mathbb{E}\left[\tau_{\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon},T}\left(\mathbf{w}_{0}\right)\right]\leq\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)+\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\text{err}(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right].

We now show that the random variable
\tau_{\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon},T}
is well-controlled.

Lemma 7.2
.

We have

\mathbb{E}\left[\tau_{\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon},T}\left(\mathbf{w}_{0}\right)\right]<\frac{T}{10}.

Proof.
Suppose otherwise that
\mathbb{E}\left[\tau_{\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon},T}\left(\mathbf{w}_{0}\right)\right]\geq\frac{T}{10}>0
. Rearranging the above gives

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda}{2A}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)}{\eta\mathbb{E}\left[\tau_{\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon},T}\left(\mathbf{w}_{0}\right)\right]}+\frac{1}{\eta\mathbb{E}\left[\tau_{\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon},T}(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right]}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\text{err}(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)}{\eta\mathbb{E}\left[\tau_{\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon},T}\left(\mathbf{w}_{0}\right)\right]}+\frac{1}{\eta\mathbb{E}\left[\tau_{\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon},T}(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right]}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\text{err}(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right].

(37)

By
Lemma
7.1
, which we may apply as our choice of
\eta
is small enough, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{2s}\right]\leq L_{2}\max(\eta T,1)^{s/2},\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}\right\|^{3s}\right]\leq L_{3}\max(\eta T,1)^{3s/4}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\text{err}(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\eta^{2}\left(2\sigma_{F}^{2}+4\max(L,B)^{2}(L_{2}\max(\eta T,1)^{s/2}+1)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+C\eta^{3}\left(8\sigma_{F}^{3}+16\max(L,B)^{3}(L_{3}\max(\eta T,1)^{3s/4}+1)\right)+2C\left(\frac{\eta d}{\beta}\right)^{3/2}

\displaystyle\leq M\left((\eta d/\beta)^{3/2}+\eta^{2}\cdot(\eta T)^{s/2}+\eta^{3}\cdot(\eta T)^{3s/4}\right).

The last line follows as
\eta T\geq 1
and from definition of
M
(recall we took
\sigma_{F}\leftarrow\max(\sigma_{F},1)
if necessary); recall

M=\max\left(\frac{1}{2},2C\right)\cdot\left(8\sigma_{F}^{3}+16\max(L,B)^{3}(\max\left(L_{2},L_{3}\right)+1)\right).

Recall our choice of
T
such that
\eta T=\frac{C_{0}}{\lambda}
, and also our choice of
C_{0}=50A\theta(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0}))\lor 1
. Therefore,
(
37
)
becomes

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda}{2A}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)}{\eta\mathbb{E}\left[\tau_{\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon},T}\left(\mathbf{w}_{0}\right)\right]}+\frac{1}{\eta\mathbb{E}\left[\tau_{\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon},T}(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right]}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\text{err}(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{10\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)}{\eta T}+\frac{10}{\eta T}\cdot T\cdot M\left((\eta d/\beta)^{3/2}+\eta^{2}\cdot(\eta T)^{s/2}+\eta^{3}\cdot(\eta T)^{3s/4}\right)

\displaystyle=10\left(\frac{\theta(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0}))\lambda}{C_{0}}+M\left(\eta^{1/2}(d/\beta)^{3/2}+\eta\cdot\frac{C_{0}^{s/2}}{\lambda^{s/2}}+\eta^{2}\cdot\frac{C_{0}^{3s/4}}{\lambda^{3s/4}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle<10\left(\frac{\lambda}{40A}+MC_{0}\left(\eta^{1/2}(d/\beta)^{3/2}+\frac{\eta}{\lambda^{s/2}}+\frac{\eta^{2}}{\lambda^{3s/4}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle<10\cdot\frac{\lambda}{20A}=\frac{\lambda}{2A}.

In the second inequality we use
\mathbb{E}\left[\tau_{\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon},T}\left(\mathbf{w}_{0}\right)\right]\geq\frac{T}{10}
which we are supposing for contradiction. The last ienquality uses

\eta\leq\min\left(\frac{1}{120^{2}A^{2}B^{2}M^{2}}\cdot\frac{\beta^{3}\lambda^{2}}{d^{3}},\frac{\lambda^{1+s/2}}{120AC_{0}M}\right).

Note as we have
\lambda\leq 1
and
A\geq 1
,
C_{0}\geq 1
,
M\geq\frac{1}{2}
, this implies

\frac{\lambda^{1+s/2}}{120AC_{0}M}\leq\frac{\lambda^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{3s}{8}}}{(120AC_{0}M)^{1/2}},

which we also use to show
MC_{0}\cdot\frac{\eta^{2}}{\lambda^{3s/4}}\leq\frac{\lambda}{120A}
. This yields contradiction, and so we have the Lemma.
∎

With
Lemma
7.2
, the finish is straightforward. By Markov’s Inequality, with probability at least 0.8,

\tau_{\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon},T}\left(\mathbf{w}_{0}\right)\leq 5\mathbb{E}\left[\tau_{\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon},T}\left(\mathbf{w}_{0}\right)\right]<\frac{T}{2}.

However,
\tau_{\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon},T}\left(\mathbf{w}_{0}\right)<T
implies
\tau_{\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon},T}\left(\mathbf{w}_{0}\right)=\tau_{\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}}\left(\mathbf{w}_{0}\right)
. Thus, with probability at least 0.8, we have
\tau_{\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}}\left(\mathbf{w}_{0}\right)<T
. That is, with probability at least 0.8 we hit
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}=\{\mathbf{w}:F(\mathbf{w})\leq\varepsilon\}
within
T
steps.

When
s=0
and
\gamma=0
, we cannot use
Lemma
7.1
anymore. But just note whenever
s=0
, we can use the upper bound
\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|^{p}\right]\leq L^{p}\leq L^{3}
for
p=2,3
in our upper bound of
\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t},\mathbf{z}_{t}}\left[\theta\left(\Phi\left(\mathbf{w}_{t+1}\right)\right)\right]
. Defining instead

\text{err}(\mathbf{w}):=\frac{1}{2}\eta^{2}\left(2\sigma_{F}^{2}+4\max(L,B)^{2}\right)+C\eta^{3}\left(8\sigma_{F}^{3}+16\max(L,B)^{3}\right)+2C\left(\frac{\eta d}{\beta}\right)^{3/2}>0,

we see the rest of the proof goes through the same, with no use of
Lemma
7.1
.

The tighter results in the case when
s=1
(the
L
-smooth and
(m,b)
-dissipative setting) are also proved identically. They follow from plugging in the uniform moment bounds from
Lemma
7.1
rather than the general ones into the proof of
Lemma
7.2
. Then,
L_{2},L_{3}
(which are different in this case) appear in the proof of
Lemma
7.2
with no
\max(\eta T,1)
term present, and again we finish the same as above.
∎

7.2
Details for Comparison to Literature

Here, we discuss how we derived optimization results using sampling results from literature, that we discussed in
Section
1
. As mentioned there, we assume an
O(1)
warm-start for all of the literature, which is the least favorable for us. Consider as an example how we obtained results for SGLD the smooth and dissipative case from
Raginsky et al. 2017
,
Xu et al. 2018
, and
Zou et al. 2021
.

Theorem 1 of
Raginsky et al. 2017
requires gradient noise
\delta
to be exponentially small in
d
, which does not make sense (we only require gradient noise of constant order, which is more realistic). Theorem 3.6, Corollary 3.7, and Remark 3.9 of
Xu et al. 2018
reports an iteration count of
K=\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon\lambda_{*}}\right)
where
\lambda_{*}
is spectral gap of the discrete-time Markov Chain given by
(
1
)
, however they do not count the iteration count
B
to compute each stochastic gradient from
B
data samples. Either they also require exponentially small gradient noise, or
B=\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{d^{6}}{\varepsilon^{4}\lambda_{*}^{4}}\right)
, and their total gradient complexity should be

K\cdot B=\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{d^{7}}{\varepsilon^{5}\lambda_{*}^{5}}\right).

Similarly, for the same paper’s claimed runtime for Stochastic Variance Reduced Gradient Langevin Dynamics (SVRG-LD) in Theorem 3.10 and Corollary 3.11, noting the correct runtime should be
K\cdot B
, we obtain a runtime of

\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{Ld^{5}}{\lambda_{*}^{4}\varepsilon^{4}}\right)\geq\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{d^{5}}{\lambda_{*}^{4}\varepsilon^{4}}\right).

The last step simply follows from noting their
L\geq 1
, being the length of an inner loop.

This accounting must also for the result Theorem 4.5 and Corollary 4.7 of
Zou et al. 2021
. Accounting for
K\cdot B
, they obtain a rate of at least
\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{d^{4}\beta^{2}}{\rho^{4}\varepsilon^{2}}\right)
, where
\rho
is the Cheeger constant of
\mu_{\beta}
, to obtain a TV distance of
\varepsilon
to the Gibbs measure. By Cheeger’s Inequality, we have
\frac{1}{\rho^{4}}\geq\textsf{C}_{\textsc{pi}}(\mu_{\beta})^{2}
.
However to convert from TV distance results to optimization results using Corollary 4.8 of their same paper
Zou et al. 2021
, we need a TV distance of
\frac{\varepsilon}{d}
(and this is necessary due to dissipativeness) to obtain an optimization result, which leads to additional dimension dependence. Combined with noting
\beta
is (at least) on the same order as
\frac{d}{\varepsilon}
up to
\log
factors, this gives a rate of at least

\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{d^{8}\textsf{C}_{\textsc{pi}}(\mu_{\beta})^{2}}{\varepsilon^{4}}\right).

for optimizing
F
to
\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{d}{\beta}+\varepsilon\right)=\widetilde{O}\left(\varepsilon\right)
tolerance..

We now discuss how we obtained results from the rest of literature. Generally the rest of literature handles exact gradients and so does not have the problem of those above two works. One point of note is that in some of the sampling literature, such as
Vempala and Wibisono 2019
;
Balasubramanian et al. 2022
;
Huang et al. 2024
, sampling is done from
e^{-f}/Z
. That is, sampling is presumed to be done at constant temperature, a different setting than optimization. In our setting
f=\beta F
, and the smoothness parameter
L
or condition number in these works is that of
f
. Thus their smoothness parameter
L
scales like
\tilde{\Omega}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)
. The rest of the rates from literature were then derived by converting KL divergence guarantees into TV distance guarantees via Pinkser’s Inequality, and then using Corollary 4.8 of
Zou et al. 2021
, analogously to the above example. In more detail, by Pinkser’s Inequality, if
F
is
s
-Hölder continuous we need KL divergence to be at most
\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{d^{s+1}}
.

Following
Remark
1.1
, it follows that the
\varepsilon
in the sampling results can be taken to be
\Theta(1)
. However, where
\varepsilon
denotes the desired optimization tolerance, the smoothness parameter
L
still scales like
\tilde{\Omega}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)
. Plugging in these choices, we obtained the results from
Section
1
.

As another example, we mention how we derived a rate from Corollary 19 of
Balasubramanian et al. 2022
(which still requires exact knowledge of gradient) in the GLD, Poincaré, and Lipschitz case. Taking
s=0
in Corollary 19 of
Balasubramanian et al. 2022
, and even supposing a warm start of
K_{0}=O(1)
is possible, we see they obtain a TV distance of
\sqrt{\varepsilon}
in

\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{\beta^{6}d^{3}\textsf{C}_{\textsc{pi}}(\mu_{\beta})^{3}}{\varepsilon^{5}}\right).

However, since
F
is Lipschitz, we require a TV distance of
\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{d}}
, the dimensionality again coming from Remark 4.6 of
Zou et al. 2021
. This yields a rate of

\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{\beta^{6}d^{8}\textsf{C}_{\textsc{pi}}(\mu_{\beta})^{3}}{\varepsilon^{10}}\right).

We must have
\beta=\widetilde{\Omega}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)
, so in this case this gives a rate of at least
\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{\textsf{C}_{\textsc{pi}}(\mu_{\beta})^{3}d^{14}}{\varepsilon^{16}}\right)
for optimizing
F
to
\widetilde{O}\left(\varepsilon\right)
tolerance. We can derive a faster rate from this result using
Remark
1.1
, which is also mentioned in
Section
1
.

Finally, we mention that we can compare the above results from
Zou et al. 2021
,
Chewi et al. 2022
, and
Balasubramanian et al. 2022
for general
\beta=\widetilde{\Omega}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)
; as mentioned in
Section
1
, to use the results of
Chewi et al. 2022
and
Balasubramanian et al. 2022
of optimization, their
\beta
dependence will be their stated dependence on the smoothness parameter
L
. Our dependence on
\beta
is always better than that of
Zou et al. 2021
, and using
\beta=\widetilde{\Omega}\left(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\right)
, we see for any such
\beta
our dependence in all parameters is better than that of
Chewi et al. 2022
;
Balasubramanian et al. 2022
when
s\leq\frac{1}{2}
.

8
Additional Proofs

8.1
Potential Argument Details

These are Lemmas from the proof of
Theorem
3.2
deferred here for the ease of presentation.

Lemma 8.1
.

Y_{t}
is a supermartingale with respect to
\mathfrak{F}_{t}
.

Proof.
This is obvious if
t+1>\tau
as then we take
Y_{t}=Y_{\tau}
. Else, suppose
t+1\leq\tau
. By
Lemma
6.2
, we have the inequality

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}}\left[\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right)|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]\leq\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)-\eta F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{t})+R(\mathbf{w}_{t},\Phi,\eta,\beta,d).

This means, since
t+1\leq\tau
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}}\left[Y_{t+1}|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}}\left[\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}{}_{t+1})\right)|\mathfrak{F}_{t}\right]+\sum_{j=0}^{t}\left(\eta F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{j})-R(\mathbf{w}_{j},\Phi,\eta,\beta,d)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)-\eta F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{t})+R(\mathbf{w}_{t},\Phi,\eta,\beta,d)+\sum_{j=0}^{t}\left(\eta F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{j})-R(\mathbf{w}_{j},\Phi,\eta,\beta,d)\right)

\displaystyle=Y_{t},

proving this part.
∎

Lemma 8.2
.

With probability at least
1-\delta
, we have

Y_{t}-Y_{0}\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}C(\eta,t,d,\beta)^{2}\right)\log(T/\delta)}

for all
1\leq t\leq T
, where

C(\eta,t,d,\beta):=4\sqrt{\theta\left(\rho_{\Phi}^{-1}\left(\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)\right)\right)}\cdot\left\|-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{\frac{2\eta}{\beta}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|+4\left\|-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{\frac{2\eta}{\beta}}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|^{2}.

Proof.
We aim to apply Azuma-Hoeffding. When
t>\tau
then
Y_{t+1}-Y_{t}=0
, so suppose
t\leq\tau
in the following. Define
C_{t}=\eta F_{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{w}_{t})-R(\mathbf{w}_{t},\Phi,\eta,\beta,d)
which is
\mathfrak{F}_{t}
-measurable and note

Y_{t+1}-Y_{t}=\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right)-\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)+C_{t}.

Let’s now bound
\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right)-\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)
from above and below. The idea here is to not Taylor expand to third order but rather second order to obtain simpler estimates; we used Taylor expansion to third order in
Lemma
6.2
to use the admissibility condition, but to establish these bounds we don’t need said condition. This is a very similar strategy as in the proof of Lemma 11 of
De Sa et al. 2022
.

For an upper bound, applying the second part of
Lemma
6.1
with
\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{w}_{t}
and
\mathbf{u}=-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}
gives by definition of
\mathbf{w}_{t+1}-\mathbf{w}_{t}
that

\displaystyle\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right)-\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t}),-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\frac{1}{2}\left\|-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\left\|\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|\left\|-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\frac{1}{2}\left\|-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)}\left\|-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\frac{1}{2}\left\|-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|^{2}.

The last step uses that from
Lemma
6.1
,

\left\|\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right\|\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\leq\rho_{2}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\sqrt{2\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)}\leq\sqrt{2\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)},

as
\rho_{2}(z)\leq\rho(z)
always holds for
z\geq 0
and by definition of
\theta^{\prime}=\frac{1}{\rho}
.
This upper bound on
\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right)-\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)
is clearly
\mathfrak{F}_{t}
measurable.

Similarly, again applying the second part of
Lemma
6.1
with
\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{w}_{t+1}
and
\mathbf{u}=\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})-\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}
gives

\displaystyle\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)-\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right)\left\langle\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1}),\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})-\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\frac{1}{2}\left\|-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right)\left\|\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right\|\left\|-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\frac{1}{2}\left\|-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|^{2}.

To make this
\mathfrak{F}_{t}
measurable and relate this to the upper bound by employing a similar strategy as in the proof of Lemma 11 of
De Sa et al. 2022
. Again we use from
Lemma
6.1
that

\left\|\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right\|\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right)\leq\rho_{2}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right)\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right)\sqrt{2\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right)}\leq\sqrt{2\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right)}.

Since
\sqrt{a+b}\leq\sqrt{\lvert a\rvert+b}\leq\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b}
for all reals
a
and
b\geq 0
with
a+b\geq 0
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right\|\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right)}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\cdot\sqrt{\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right)-\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)+\theta\left(\Phi(w_{t})\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2}\left(\sqrt{\theta\left(\Phi(w_{t})\right)}+\sqrt{\left\lvert\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right)-\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\right\rvert}\right).

Using this we have

\displaystyle\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right)-\theta\left(\Phi(w_{t})\right)

\displaystyle\geq-\theta^{\prime}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right)\left\|\nabla\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right\|\left\|-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt-\frac{1}{2}\left\|-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq-\sqrt{2\left\lvert\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right)-\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\right\rvert}\left\|-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt-\sqrt{2\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)}\left\|-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt-\frac{1}{2}\left\|-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|^{2}.

By AM-GM we have

\displaystyle\sqrt{2\left\lvert\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right)-\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\right\rvert}\left\|-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\left\lvert\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right)-\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\right\rvert+2\left\|-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|^{2}}{2}.

Using this gives

\displaystyle\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right)-\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{\left\lvert\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1}\right)-\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)\right\rvert}{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt-\sqrt{2\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)}\left\|-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt-\frac{3}{2}\left\|-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|^{2}.

Doing cases on the sign of
\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right)-\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)
, we get that in all cases

\displaystyle\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right)-\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)

\displaystyle\geq-2\sqrt{2\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)}\left\|-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt-3\left\|-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|^{2}.

This yields a lower bound on
\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right)-\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)
that is
\mathfrak{F}_{t}
-measurable.

Now, to finish the setup for the concentration bound via Azuma-Hoeffding, we just need to upper bound the difference between these bounds. The above shows that this difference is at most

\displaystyle\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t+1})\right)-\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)

\displaystyle\leq 4\sqrt{\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)}\left\|-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|+4\left\|-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 4\sqrt{\theta\left(\rho_{\Phi}^{-1}\left(\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)\right)\right)}\left\|-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+4\left\|-\eta\nabla F(\mathbf{w}_{t})+\sqrt{2\eta/\beta}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\right\|^{2}.

The last step follows since
t\leq\tau
and since
\rho_{\Phi}
is increasing, we have by definition of
\tau
that

\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\leq\rho_{\Phi}^{-1}\left(\kappa^{\prime}\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)\right).

Now by
Lemma
6.1
, we know
\theta
is increasing so

\sqrt{\theta\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{t})\right)}\leq\sqrt{\theta\left(\rho_{\Phi}^{-1}\left(\kappa\rho_{\Phi}\left(\Phi(\mathbf{w}_{0})\right)\right)\right)}.

As defined earlier, we denote the above expression by
C(\eta,t,d,\beta,\rho)\geq 0
for convenience. This serves as an upper bound regardless of whether
t\leq\tau
by the initial discussion. Thus, applying Azuma Hoeffding, which we may apply by
Lemma
8.1
and since
Y_{t+1}-Y_{t}=\theta\left(\Phi\left(\mathbf{w}_{t+1}\right)\right)-\theta\left(\Phi\left(\mathbf{w}_{t}\right)\right)
, gives with probability at least
1-\frac{\delta}{T}
we have that

Y_{t}-Y_{0}\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{t=0}^{t-1}C(\eta,t,d,\beta,\rho)^{2}\right)\log\left(T/\delta\right)},

and we conclude via Union Bound.
∎

8.2
Additional Helper Results

Here we establish many of the results we used in the main discretization proofs.

Lemma 8.3
.

Suppose
F
satisfies
Assumption
2.2
. Then for all
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w})\right\|\leq L\max(1,\left\|\mathbf{w}^{\star}\right\|)^{s}\left(\left\|\mathbf{w}\right\|^{s}+1\right),

where
\mathbf{w}^{\star}
is any global minima of
F
. Moreover, if
Assumption
2.5
holds, the above also holds for the stochastic gradient estimates
\left\|\nabla f(\mathbf{w};\mathbf{z})\right\|
.

Proof.
Note
\nabla F(\mathbf{w}^{\star})=0
. By Triangle Inequality and
Assumption
2.2
,

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w})\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w})-\nabla F(\mathbf{w}^{\star})\right\|

\displaystyle\leq L\left\|\mathbf{w}-\mathbf{w}^{\star}\right\|^{s}

\displaystyle\leq L\left(\left\|\mathbf{w}\right\|+\left\|\mathbf{w}^{\star}\right\|\right)^{s}

\displaystyle\leq L\max(1,\left\|\mathbf{w}^{\star}\right\|)^{s}\left(\left\|\mathbf{w}\right\|^{s}+1\right).

The last two steps used the following elementary inequalities:

(az+b)^{s}\leq\max(a,b)^{s}(z+1)^{s}\text{ for all }{}a,b,z\geq 0.

(z+1)^{1/s^{\prime}}\leq z^{1/s^{\prime}}+1\iff z+1\leq(z^{1/s^{\prime}}+1)^{s^{\prime}}\text{ for all }{}s^{\prime}\geq 1.

The extension to stochastic gradients given
Assumption
2.5
is immediate.
∎

The following result is used to control the values of
F
using
Assumption
2.2
.

Lemma 8.4
.

Suppose
F
satisfies
Assumption
2.2
. Then for all
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

F(\mathbf{w})\leq L\left\|\mathbf{w}-\mathbf{w}^{\star}\right\|^{s+1}.

Proof.
The proof is very similar to Lemma 3.4 of
Bubeck et al. 2015
. Let
\mathbf{w}^{\star}
be any global minima of
F
, thus
F(\mathbf{w}^{\star})=0
and
\nabla F(\mathbf{w}^{\star})=0
. We see from calculus and Cauchy-Schwartz that

\displaystyle F(\mathbf{w})

\displaystyle=\left\lvert F(\mathbf{w})-F(\mathbf{w}^{\star})-\left\langle\nabla F(\mathbf{w}^{\star}),\mathbf{w}-\mathbf{w}^{\star}\right\rangle\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\int_{t=0}^{1}\left\langle\nabla F(\mathbf{w}^{\star}+t(\mathbf{w}-\mathbf{w}^{\star}))-\nabla F(\mathbf{w}^{\star}),\mathbf{w}-\mathbf{w}^{\star}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}t\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert\int_{t=0}^{1}\left\|\nabla F(\mathbf{w}^{\star}+t(\mathbf{w}-\mathbf{w}^{\star}))-\nabla F(\mathbf{w}^{\star})\right\|\left\|\mathbf{w}-\mathbf{w}^{\star}\right\|\mathrm{d}t\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert\int_{t=0}^{1}Lt^{s}\left\|\mathbf{w}-\mathbf{w}^{\star}\right\|^{s}\left\|\mathbf{w}-\mathbf{w}^{\star}\right\|\mathrm{d}t\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq L\left\|\mathbf{w}-\mathbf{w}^{\star}\right\|^{s+1},

where we apply Cauchy-Schwartz to obtain the first inequality and
Assumption
2.2
for the second.
∎

We also need the following simple integral to prove
Lemma
5.1
.

Lemma 8.5
.

We have for any
0\leq s\leq 1
and
M\geq 0
that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-M\left\|\mathbf{w}\right\|^{s+1}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{w}=\frac{2\pi^{d/2}}{\Gamma(d/2)}\cdot\frac{1}{s+1}\cdot M^{-\frac{d}{s+1}}\cdot\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{s+1}\right).

Proof.
The surface area of
\mathcal{S}^{d-1}
is
\frac{2\pi^{d/2}}{\Gamma(d/2)}
, which scales by
r^{d-1}
for an arbitrary radius
r
. Consider partitioning
\mathbb{R}^{d}
into spheres of radius
r
: upon making this change of variables, which formally is
\mathrm{d}\mathbf{w}=\frac{2\pi^{d/2}}{\Gamma(d/2)}r^{d-1}\mathrm{d}r
, we obtain

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-M\left\|\mathbf{w}\right\|^{s+1}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{w}=\frac{2\pi^{d/2}}{\Gamma(d/2)}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-Mr^{s+1}}r^{d-1}\mathrm{d}r.

Let
u=r^{s+1}
, therefore
r=u^{\frac{1}{s+1}}
and
\mathrm{d}r=\frac{1}{s+1}u^{-\frac{s}{s+1}}\mathrm{d}u
. Thus

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-M\left\|\mathbf{w}\right\|^{s+1}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{w}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi^{d/2}}{\Gamma(d/2)}\cdot\frac{1}{s+1}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-Mu}u^{\frac{d-1-s}{s+1}}\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi^{d/2}}{\Gamma(d/2)}\cdot\frac{1}{s+1}\cdot M^{-\frac{d}{s+1}}\cdot\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{s+1}\right).

Here, the last equality is a well known integral essentially following from definition of the Gamma function, specifically

M^{-t}\Gamma(t)=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-Mu}u^{t-1}\mathrm{d}u.

It follows since
d\geq 1\geq s
and
s\geq 0
, hence
\frac{d-1-s}{s+1}=\frac{d}{s+1}-1\geq-1
, so we may apply these results regarding the Gamma function.
∎

The last lemma is used to upper bound
z^{p}+1
for all
z\geq 0
and any
p\geq 0
.

Lemma 8.6
.

For all
z\geq 0
and any
p\geq 0
,
z^{p}+1\leq 2(z+1)^{p}
.

Proof.
First suppose
p\geq 1
. Here we show
z^{p}+1\leq(z+1)^{p}
, which clearly suffices. Letting
f(z)=(z+1)^{p}-(z^{p}+1)
, we see
f^{\prime}(z)\geq 0
always. Therefore
f(z)\geq f(0)=0
, proving this case.

Now suppose
0\leq p<1
. Let
f(z)=\frac{(z+1)^{p}}{z^{p}+1}
. Then,

f^{\prime}(z)=\frac{p(z+1)^{p-1}\cdot(z^{p}+1)-(z+1)^{p}\cdot pz^{p-1}}{(z^{p}+1)^{2}}=\frac{p(z+1)^{p-1}\left(1-z^{p-1}\right)}{(z^{p}+1)^{2}}.

Therefore
f^{\prime}(z)\leq 0
for
z\in[0,1]
and
f^{\prime}(z)\geq 0
for
z\in[1,\infty)
, so
f(z)
is minimized on
[0,\infty)
when
z=1
. Hence,
f(z)\geq f(1)=2^{p-1}
. Thus,
z^{p}+1\leq 2^{1-p}(z+1)^{p}\leq 2(z+1)^{p}
as desired.
∎
